.
Since
\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}
is always larger than one, we see that
2
is a stronger assumption than each term in
Theorem

A.2
being smaller than one.

For ease of interpretation, when all
l
have
\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}\asymp 1
, and
\theta_{\max}^{(l)}\asymp\theta_{\min}^{(l)}\asymp\sqrt{\rho_{n}}
and
K\asymp 1
, we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n\rho_{n}}};

\displaystyle\alpha_{\max}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\log(n)}{n\rho_{n}};

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{n\rho_{n}}.

Therefore, the
\sin\mathbf{\Theta}
bound simplifies to

\displaystyle\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\sqrt{Ln\rho_{n}}}+\frac{\log(n)}{n\rho_{n}}.

This final bound shows that
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}
concentrates in
\sin\mathbf{\Theta}
distance about
\mathbf{U}
as
n
increases by a factor that improves with
\sqrt{L}
when
L\lesssim n\rho_{n}/\log(n)
. For a single stochastic blockmodel without degree corrections, the
\sin\mathbf{\Theta}
distance between
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}
and
\mathbf{U}
can be upper bounded as
\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n\rho_{n}}}

(
Lei and Rinaldo 2015
)
. Therefore,
Theorem

A.2
, which utilizes the information from all the networks and allows degree heterogeneity, already demonstrates improvement from multiple networks by a factor of
\max\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}},\frac{\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\sqrt{n\rho_{n}}}\}
relative to the single-network setting. However, it is important to emphasize that a primary benefit of this second stage aggregation is to ameliorate degree heterogeneity, which is not reflected in the homogeneous degree setting.

A.3
Second Stage Characterization II: Asymptotic Expansion

In essence, we require
Theorem

A.2
to demonstrate that the clusters are correctly identified (see the proof of
Theorem

3.1
in
Section

A.4
), but it falls short of providing a fine-grained characterization for the rows of
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}
, which is what is needed for the exponential error rate.

The following result demonstrates a first-order asymptotic expansion for the singular vectors in the second stage of our algorithm. The proof is given in
Section

A.3
.

Theorem A.3

(Asymptotic Expansion: Stage II)
.

Suppose the conditions of
Theorem

3.1
hold. Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{*}:

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{O}(K)}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}\|_{F}.

There is an event
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}
satisfying
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}\big)\geq 1-O(n^{-10})
such that on this event, we have the asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{U}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}+\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}},

where
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
is the operator from
Theorem

A.1
and the residual satisfies

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{nL\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)}{L^{2}\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}+\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}.

Here
\alpha_{\max}
is as
Theorem

A.2
. In particular, under the assumptions of
Theorem

3.1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16\sqrt{n_{\max}}}.

Theorem

A.3
establishes a first-order expansion for the rows of the difference matrix
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{U}
, which is the main technical tool required to establish
Theorem

3.1
. The proof of
Theorem

A.3
relies on both
Theorem

A.1
and
Theorem

A.2
, but requires a number of additional considerations to bound the residual term
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}
in
\ell_{2,\infty}
norm.

A.4
Proof of Theorem
3.1
and Theorem
3.2

With all of these ingredients in place, we are nearly prepared to prove
Theorem

3.1
. In the proof we will also require several results concerning the population parameters, which we state in the following two lemmas.
The proofs can be found in
Appendix

B
.

Lemma A.1

(Population Properties: Stage I)
.

Suppose
1
holds, and let
\lambda_{r}^{(l)}(\mathbf{P}^{(l)})
denote the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{P}^{(l)}
and let
\lambda_{r}(\mathbf{B}^{(l)})
denote the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{B}^{(l)}
. Then for all
1\leq r\leq K
,

\displaystyle\theta_{i}^{(l)}\lesssim\|\mathbf{X}^{(l)}_{i\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\theta_{i}^{(l)}{\sqrt{K}};

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{U}^{(l)}_{i\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{K}\frac{\theta^{(l)}_{i}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|};

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}^{(l)}(\mathbf{P}^{(l)})

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}{K}\lambda_{r}(\mathbf{B}^{(l)}).

Next, the following result establishes the population properties of the the second stage; in particular demonstrating a lower bound on the smallest eigenvalue of the population matrix
\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}
in terms of
\bar{\lambda}
.

Lemma A.2

(Population Properties: Stage II)
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{Y}
is rank
K
, and let
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbf{U}\Sigma\mathbf{V}^{\top}
be its (rank
K
) singular value decomposition. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{M},

where
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
is some invertible matrix satisfying

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{s\cdot}\|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n_{r}^{-1}+n_{s}^{-1}}.

In addition, when
n_{\min}\asymp n_{\max}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lambda_{Y}^{2}:

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\min}\bigg(\sum_{l}\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\bigg)\gtrsim\frac{n}{K}L\bar{\lambda}.

Armed with these lemmas as well as Theorems
A.1
,
A.2
, and
A.3
, we are prepared to prove
Theorem

3.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

We follow the analysis technique developed in
Jin et al. 2022b
to derive an exponential rate for the output of
(1+\varepsilon)

K
-means.
First will use the the
\sin\mathbf{\Theta}
bound (
Theorem

A.2
) together with Lemma 5.3 of
Lei and Rinaldo 2015
to demonstrate a Hamming error of order strictly less than
\frac{n_{\min}}{4}
, so that each cluster has at a majority of its true members. This allows us to associate each empirical cluster centroid to a true cluster centroid. Next, we will study the empirical centroids of these clusters to show that they are strictly closer to their corresponding true cluster centroid than they are to each other. Finally, we decompose the expected error into individual node-wise errors, where we apply the asymptotic expansion in
Theorem

A.3
to obtain the exponential error rate.

In what follows, let
\mathcal{E}_{\sin\mathbf{\Theta}}
denote the event

\displaystyle\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|\leq\frac{\beta}{8K\sqrt{C_{\varepsilon}}},

where
\beta\in(0,1)
is such that
n_{\min}\geq\beta n_{\max}
and
C_{\varepsilon}
is a constant to be defined in the subsequent analysis. We note that by
Theorem

A.2
the event
\mathcal{E}_{\sin\mathbf{\Theta}}
holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
. We also let
(\mathbf{\widehat{Z}},\mathbf{\widehat{M}})
denote the output of
(1+\varepsilon)

K
-means on the rows of
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}
, where
\mathbf{\widehat{Z}}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times K}
and
\mathbf{\widehat{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
.

Step 1: Initial Hamming Error

First by
Lemma

A.2
it holds that
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{ZM}
where
\mathbf{M}
has
K
unique rows satisfying

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\max}}}\leq\|\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{s\cdot}\|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n_{\min}}}.

Define the matrix

\mathbf{\widehat{V}}:=\mathbf{\widehat{Z}}\mathbf{\widehat{M}}
. Define

S_{r}:=\{i\in\mathcal{C}(r):\|\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{V}}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}\|\geq\delta_{r}/2\}
, where
\delta_{r}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{r}}}
. By Lemma 5.3 of
Lei and Rinaldo 2015
, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{z}(i)\neq z(i)\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{r=1}^{K}|S_{r}|\leq\sum_{r=1}^{K}\frac{|S_{r}|}{n_{r}}=\sum_{r=1}^{K}|S_{r}|\delta_{r}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}\|_{F}^{2}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|_{F}^{2}\leq C_{\varepsilon}K\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|^{2}.

Therefore, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{\sin\mathbf{\Theta}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{z}(i)\neq z(i)\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}Kn\frac{\beta^{2}}{64K^{2}C_{\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{\beta}{64}n_{\min},

since
n\leq Kn_{\max}\leq\frac{K}{\beta}n_{\min}
. Therefore, since this error is strictly less than
\beta n_{\min}/64\leq n_{\min}n_{r}/(64n_{\max})
, each cluster
r
has at least
n_{r}-\beta n_{\min}/64\geq(1-n_{\min}/(64n_{\max}))n_{r}\geq(63/64)n_{r}
of its true members. This implies that we can associate each empirical cluster to a true cluster – let these empirical clusters be denoted
\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)
. Observe that we must have that
|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|\geq(1-\beta/64)n_{\min}
and that
|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)\setminus\mathcal{C}(r)|\leq\beta n_{\min}/64
.

Step 2: Properties of Empirical Centroids

Recall that the cluster centroid associated to
\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)
is equal to
\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{r\cdot}
. Then by definition,

\displaystyle\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{r\cdot}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i\cdot}.

Recall that
\mathbf{U}
consists of
K
unique rows of
\mathbf{M}
. Without loss of generality assume that
\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot}
is associated to
\mathcal{C}(r)
. Then

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{r\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot}\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|}\big\|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)}(\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot})\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|}\big\|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)}(\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot})\big\|+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|}\big\|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)}(\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot})\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|}\bigg\|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)}(\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot})\bigg\|+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|}\bigg\|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)\setminus\mathcal{C}(r)}(\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot})\bigg\|.

We observe that for
i\notin\mathcal{C}(r)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\max}}}\leq\|\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot}\|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n_{\min}}}

by
Lemma

A.2
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{r\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|}\bigg\|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)}(\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot})\bigg\|+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|}\bigg\|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)\setminus\mathcal{C}(r)}(\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot})\bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|^{1/2}}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{U}\|_{F}+\frac{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)\setminus\mathcal{C}(r)|}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n_{\min}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2K}}{\sqrt{n_{\min}(1-\beta/64)}}\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|+\frac{\beta n_{\min}}{64(1-\beta/64)n_{\min}}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n_{\min}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2K}}{\sqrt{\beta n_{\max}(1-\beta/64)}}\frac{\beta}{8KC_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}}+\frac{\beta}{64(1-\beta/64)}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{\beta n_{\max}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\max}}}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2\beta}}{8K^{1/2}C_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\sqrt{1-\beta/64}}+\frac{\beta^{1/2}\sqrt{2}}{64(1-\beta/64)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8\sqrt{n_{\max}}},

since
n_{\min}\geq\beta n_{\max}
,
K\geq 1
and
\beta<1
, as well as the assumption
C_{\varepsilon}\geq 4
. Therefore, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{\sin\mathbf{\Theta}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq r\leq K}\|\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{r\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot}\|\leq\frac{1}{8\sqrt{n_{\max}}}.

Step 3: Applying The Asymptotic Expansion

In this section we will use the previous bound on the cluster centroids and
Theorem

A.3
to obtain the desired bound. Recall that by
Theorem

A.2
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\sin\mathbf{\Theta}}^{c})=O(n^{-10})
. It then holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\ell(\widehat{z},z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Z}_{i\cdot}\neq\mathbf{\widehat{Z}}_{i\cdot})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Z}_{i\cdot}\neq\mathbf{\widehat{Z}}_{i\cdot},\mathcal{E}_{\sin\mathbf{\Theta}})+O(n^{-10}).

Suppose that
\|(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top})_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}\|\leq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{n_{\max}}},
and suppose the
i
’th node is in community
r
. Then on the event
\mathcal{E}_{\sin\mathbf{\Theta}}

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{r\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top})_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}\|+\|\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{r\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{n_{\max}}}+\max_{s}\|\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{s\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{s\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{8\sqrt{n_{\max}}}.

In addition, for any
s\neq r
, we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{s\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\geq\|\mathbf{M}_{r\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{s\cdot}\|-\|\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}\|-\|\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{s\cdot}-\mathbf{M}_{s\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\max}}}-\frac{1}{4\sqrt{n_{\max}}}-\frac{1}{8\sqrt{n_{\max}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{5}{8\sqrt{n_{\max}}}.

Therefore, node
i
must belong to cluster
\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)
, so that there is no error on node
i
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Z}_{i\cdot}\neq\mathbf{\widehat{Z}}_{i\cdot},\mathcal{E}_{\sin\mathbf{\Theta}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\|(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top})_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}\|\geq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{n_{\max}}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\|(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top})_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}\|\geq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{n_{\max}}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}})+O(n^{-10}),

where
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}
is the event in
Theorem

A.3
. On the event
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{U}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}+\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}},

with

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16\sqrt{n_{\max}}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top})_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}\|\geq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{n_{\max}}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\bigg\|\geq\frac{1}{8\sqrt{n_{\max}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\bigg\|\geq C\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq K\max_{k}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigg|\sum_{l}e_{i}^{\top}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})\big(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}e_{k}\bigg|\geq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg).

We will apply the Bernstein inequality now. We have that

\displaystyle\sum_{l}e_{i}^{\top}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}e_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\sum_{j}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})_{ij}\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\bigg)_{jk}.

Using
Lemma

A.1
and
Lemma

A.2
, the variance
v
of this quantity is upper bounded by

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{j}^{(l)}\|e_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{j}^{(l)}\|e_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{U}^{(l)}\|^{2}\||\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\|^{2}\|\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\|^{2}\|(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{j}^{(l)}\frac{K(\theta_{j}^{(l)})^{2}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}\frac{K}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot}\|^{2}}\frac{nK^{2}}{n^{2}L^{2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{K^{4}}{nL^{2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{j}^{(l)}\frac{(\theta_{j}^{(l)})^{2}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}(\theta_{i}^{(l)})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{K^{4}}{nL^{2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\sum_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{3}^{3}}{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda^{(l)}_{\min}}.

In addition, each term satisfies

\displaystyle\max_{l,j}\|e_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{l,j}C\frac{\theta_{j}^{(l)}K}{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\frac{K}{\sqrt{n}L\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{K^{2}}{\sqrt{n}L\bar{\lambda}}\max_{l}\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}.

By Bernstein’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{l}e_{i}^{\top}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})\big(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}e_{k}\bigg|\geq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\bigg(-\frac{C^{2}\frac{1}{128n}}{C_{1}\frac{K^{4}}{nL^{2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\sum_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{3}^{3}}{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda^{(l)}_{\min}}+C_{1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{K^{2}}{\sqrt{n}L\bar{\lambda}}\max_{l}\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\bigg(-\frac{C_{1}}{C_{2}\frac{K^{4}}{L^{2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\sum_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{3}^{3}}{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda^{(l)}_{\min}}+\frac{1}{24}C_{2}\frac{K^{2}}{L\bar{\lambda}}\max_{l}\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\Bigg(-C_{3}\min\bigg\{\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{2}L}{K^{4}}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{3}^{3}}{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}\bigg)^{-1},\frac{L\bar{\lambda}}{K^{2}}\min_{m}\frac{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}\bigg\}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\Bigg(-cL\min\bigg\{\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{2}}{K^{4}\mathrm{err}_{\mathrm{ave}}^{(i)}},\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{K^{2}\mathrm{err}_{\max}^{(i)}}\bigg\}\Bigg),

where
\mathrm{err}_{\mathrm{ave}}^{(i)}
and
\mathrm{err}_{\max}^{(i)}
are as defined in Eq (
3.2
). This completes the proof.
∎

A.4.1
Proof of Theorem
3.2

Proof of
Theorem

3.2
.

The proof proceeds from partway through the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. We have already shown that on the event
\mathcal{E}_{\sin\mathbf{\Theta}}
if
\|(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top})_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}\|\leq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{n_{\max}}}
then node
i
must be classified correctly.
By repeating the argument in step 3 of the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Z}_{i\cdot}\neq\mathbf{\widehat{Z}}_{i\cdot})\leq 2K\exp\bigg(-cL\min\bigg\{\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{2}}{K^{4}\mathrm{err}_{\mathrm{ave}}^{(i)}},\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{K^{2}\mathrm{err}_{\max}^{(i)}}\bigg\}\bigg)+O(n^{-10}).

In order for the exponential to be strictly less than
O(n^{-10})
, we require that

\displaystyle\min\bigg\{\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{2}}{K^{4}\mathrm{err}_{\mathrm{ave}}^{(i)}},\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{K^{2}\mathrm{err}_{\max}^{(i)}}\bigg\}\geq\frac{C\log(n)}{L},

where
C
is a sufficiently large constant. Recalling the definitions of
\mathrm{err}_{\mathrm{ave}}^{(i)}
and
\mathrm{err}_{\max}^{(i)}
, we see that we must have

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{K^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{C\log(n)}{L}\max_{l}\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}}\frac{1}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}};

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{2}}{K^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{C\log(n)}{L}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{3}^{3}}{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}\bigg).

Considering the first term and rearranging, we see that we require that

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{K^{2}}\min_{l}\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}}{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}\bigg)\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}\geq\frac{C\log(n)}{L}.

A sufficient condition is that

\displaystyle\min_{l}\mathrm{SNR}_{l}^{2}\geq\frac{CK^{8}\log(n)}{L\bar{\lambda}}

As for the second term, by upper bounding
\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{3}^{3}\leq\theta_{\max}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}
, we see that it sufficient to have that

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{2}}{K^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{C\log(n)}{L}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}}\bigg)\frac{1}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}\bigg).

(A.2)

Therefore, rearranging (
A.2
) yields the sufficient condition

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}\frac{1}{\mathrm{SNR}_{l}^{2}}\bigg)^{-1}\geq C\frac{K^{8}\log(n)}{L\bar{\lambda}^{2}}.

It is straightforward to check that the condition in
Theorem

3.2
is sufficient for the result to hold.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs of Identifiability and Algorithm Recovery Results

In this section we prove
Theorem

2.1
and
Proposition

2.1
, as well as
Lemma

A.1
and
Lemma

A.2
.

B.1
Proof of Theorem
2.1

Proof of
Theorem

2.1
.

We first prove the “if” direction. Suppose for contradiction that there is another block membership matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{Z}}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times K^{\prime}}
with at least one vertex assigned to each community, and positive diagonal matrices

\{\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}
and symmetric matrices
\{\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}

such that

{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{B}}^{(l)}{\mathbf{Z}}^{\top}{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}=\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{(l)}\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\quad\quad\quad\text{for each $l\in[L]$.}

Equivalently, since the matrices
\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}
have positive diagonal, for all
l\in[L]
it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{(l)}\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}^{\top}=

\displaystyle[\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}]^{-1}{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{B}}^{(l)}{\mathbf{Z}}^{\top}[\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}]^{-1}{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{B}}^{(l)}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z})^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{V}}^{(l)}\mathbf{D}^{(l)}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{V}^{(l)})^{\top}.

(B.1)

For any vertex index
i\in[n]
, denote by
z(i)
and
\tilde{z}(i)
the community memberships according to
\mathbf{Z}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}
. We will show that
K^{\prime}\geq K
and if
K^{\prime}=K
then
z(i)=z(j)
if and only if
\tilde{z}(i)=\tilde{z}(j)
.

By the RHS of Eq. (
B.1
), the column space of
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}
should be contained within the column space of
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}
(as these two matrices are full rank by construction), and hence, there is a matrix
\mathbf{M}^{(l)}\in\real^{K^{\prime}\times K_{l}}
such that

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}=\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{M}^{(l)},\quad\quad\text{for all }l\in[L].

(B.2)

In particular, this implies that for any
i\in[n]
,

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}_{ii}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{z(i)\cdot}=\mathbf{M}^{(l)}_{\tilde{z}(i)\cdot}\quad\quad\text{for all }l\in[L].

(B.3)

If
\tilde{z}(i)=\tilde{z}(j)
then

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}_{ii}\mathbf{V}_{z(i)\cdot}^{(l)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}_{jj}\mathbf{V}_{z(j)\cdot}^{(l)}\quad\quad\text{for all }l\in[L].

This equation implies that the normalized rows are the same, i.e.,
\mathbf{Q}_{z(i)\cdot}^{(l)}=\mathbf{Q}_{z(j)\cdot}^{(l)}
for all
l\in[L]
, and hence

\mathbf{Q}_{z(i)\cdot}=\mathbf{Q}_{z(j)\cdot}
, which is only possible if
z(i)=z(j)
according to the condition in the proposition.

Now, take a set of vertices
\mathcal{T}\subset[n]
such that each vertex is in a different community according to
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathcal{T}\cdot}=I
, and hence, Eq. (
B.2
) implies

(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{V}^{(l)})_{\mathcal{T}\cdot}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{T}\cdot}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{z(\mathcal{T})\cdot}=\mathbf{M}^{(l)}\quad\quad\text{for all }l\in[L].

If there are two indexes
i,j\in\mathcal{T}
such that
z(i)=z(j)
, then the corresponding rows of
\mathbf{M}^{(l)}
are proportional, that is
\mathbf{M}_{z(i)\cdot}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{ii}^{(l)}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{z(i)\cdot}
and
\mathbf{M}_{z(j)\cdot}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{jj}^{(l)}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{z(i)\cdot}
for all
l\in[L]
. If
K^{\prime}=K
, this implies that
\mathbf{M}^{(l)}
can only have at most
K-1
different rows that are not proportional, and these are the same for all
l\in[L]
. Hence, by Eq. (
B.3
) the matrix
\mathbf{Q}
has at most
K-1
different rows, which contradicts the assumption. Note that this is also the case if
K^{\prime}<K
. If
K^{\prime}>K
, then it is still possible to have
z(i)=z(j)
, but then
\mathbf{Z}
can fit the same model with fewer communities.

We now prove the “only if” direction. Suppose for contradiction that
\mathbf{Q}
has repeated rows; we will construct
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\mathbf{B}^{(l)}
that yield the same
\mathbf{P}^{(l)}
matrices. Without loss of generality we may assume that rows one and two are repeated, since communities are identifiable up to permutation. Furthermore, without loss of generality we can have
\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}=\mathbf{V}^{(l)}
. Indeed, for
i\in\mathcal{C}(r)
, we can rescale
\theta_{i}^{(l)}
via

\theta_{i}^{(l)}\mapsto\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{r\cdot}\|
, which still yields the same matrix
\mathbf{P}^{(l)}
since

\mathbf{P}^{(l)}_{ij}=\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{j}^{(l)}(\mathbf{V}^{(l)}\mathbf{D}^{(l)}\mathbf{V}^{(l)})^{\top}_{z(i)z(j)}=\big(\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{z(i)\cdot}\|\big)\big(\theta_{j}^{(l)}\|\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{z(j)\cdot}\|\big)\frac{(\mathbf{V}^{(l)}\mathbf{D}^{(l)}\mathbf{V}^{(l)})^{\top}_{z(i)z(j)}}{\|\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{z(i)\cdot}\|\|\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{z(j)\cdot}\|}.

Therefore, the first two rows of
\mathbf{V}^{(l)}
are repeated for all
l
. However, this implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}^{(l)}_{1r}=\big(\mathbf{V}^{(l)}\mathbf{D}^{(l)}(\mathbf{V}^{(l)})^{\top}\big)_{1r}=\sum_{s=1}^{K_{l}}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{1s}\mathbf{D}^{(l)}_{ss}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{rs}=\sum_{s=1}^{K_{l}}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{2s}\mathbf{D}^{(l)}_{ss}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}_{rs}=\mathbf{B}^{(l)}_{2r},

which shows that the first row and column of
\mathbf{B}^{(l)}
is repeated. Therefore, we can collapse the first two communities into one community, creating a new matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{(l)}
with
K-1
communities (with the first two communities merged). Then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{(l)}_{ij}=\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{j}^{(l)}\mathbf{B}^{(l)}_{z(i)z(j)}=\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{j}^{(l)}\mathbf{B}^{(l)}_{\tilde{z}(i)\tilde{z}(j)},

which shows that
\mathbf{Z}
is not identifiable unless
\mathbf{Q}
has no repeated rows.
∎

B.2
Proof of Proposition
2.1

Proof of
Proposition

2.1
.

We will demonstrate that the left singular vectors obtained immediately before clustering contain exactly
K
unique rows, for which the final result follows. We will analyze each stage separately.

First Stage (individual network embedding):
First, suppose that
\mathbf{P}^{(l)}=\mathbf{U}^{(l)}\Lambda^{(l)}(\mathbf{U}^{(l)})^{\top}
with
\mathbf{U}\in\real^{n\times K_{l}}
is the eigendecomposition of
\mathbf{P}^{(l)}
, and let
\mathbf{B}^{(l)}=\mathbf{V}^{(l)}\mathbf{D}^{(l)}(\mathbf{V}^{(l)})^{\top}
be the eigendecomposition of
\mathbf{B}^{(l)}
, with
\mathbf{V}\in\real^{K\times K_{l}}
a matrix with orthogonal columns and
\mathbf{D}^{(l)}\in\real^{K_{l}\times K_{l}}
a diagonal matrix with non-zero elements in the diagonal. From this factorization it is evident that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{B}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}\mathbf{D}^{(l)}(\mathbf{V}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}.

Since
\mathbf{U}^{(l)}
and
\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}
are full rank matrices, they have the same column space, so

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{V}^{(l)}\mathbf{H}^{(l)},

where
\mathbf{H}^{(l)}\in\real^{K_{l}\times K_{l}}
is a full rank matrix. From this decomposition it is immediate that
\mathbf{U}^{(l)}
consists of rows of
\mathbf{V}^{(l)}\mathbf{H}^{(l)}
with each row of
\mathbf{U}^{(l)}
scaled by
\theta_{i}^{(l)}
. Let
\xi_{r}^{(l)}
denote the
r
’th row of
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{H}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2}
. Then if
z(i)=r
,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2})_{i\cdot}

\displaystyle=\theta_{i}^{(l)}\xi_{r}^{(l)}=\theta_{i}^{(l)}\mathbf{V}_{r\cdot}^{(l)}\mathbf{H}^{(l)},

and hence

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{i\cdot}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\xi_{r}^{(l)}}{\|\theta_{i}^{(l)}\xi_{r}^{(l)}\|}=\frac{1}{\|\xi_{r}^{(l)}\|}\xi_{r}^{(l)}=\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{V}_{r\cdot}^{(l)}\mathbf{H}^{(l)}\|}\mathbf{V}_{r\cdot}^{(l)}\mathbf{H}^{(l)},

which does not depend on
\theta_{i}^{(l)}
.

Second Stage (joint network embedding)
: We now consider the left singular vectors of the matrix
\mathcal{Y}
defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{Y}^{(1)},\cdots,\mathbf{Y}^{(L)}].

Observe that the leading
K
left singular vectors
\mathbf{U}
of
\mathcal{Y}
are given by the leading
K
eigenvectors of the matrix
\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}
, which can equivalently be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}.

Consider
i
and
j
in community
r
and
s
respectively. Then from the analysis in the previous step,

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}\big)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\frac{\langle\xi_{r}^{(l)},\xi_{s}^{(l)}\rangle}{\|\xi_{r}^{(l)}\|\|\xi_{s}^{(l)}\|}.

Consequently, this shows that
\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}
is a matrix of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Z}\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{L}(\Xi^{(l)})(\Xi^{(l)})^{\top}\bigg)\mathbf{Z}^{\top}=(\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{\Xi})(\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{\Xi})^{\top},

where
\Xi^{(l)}
is the matrix whose rows are
\xi_{r}^{(l)}/\|\xi_{r}^{(l)}\|
and
\mathbf{\Xi}=[\Xi^{(1)}\cdots\Xi^{(l)}]
. Next observe that

\Xi^{(l)}=\mathbf{D}^{(l)}\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}\mathbf{H}^{(l)}=\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}\mathbf{M}^{(l)}

for some matrix
\mathbf{M}^{(l)}
that is full rank, where
\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}
is as in
Theorem

2.1
. Since
\mathbf{Q}
has
K
different rows (by assumption),
\mathbf{\Xi}
has
K
different rows, and hence
\mathbf{\Xi}\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}
is a
K\times K
block matrix. Let
\mathbf{U}
denote the leading
\tilde{K}
eigenvectors of
\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}
, where
\tilde{K}
is the rank of
\mathcal{Y}
. Let
\mathbf{V\Gamma V}^{\top}
denote the eigendecomposiion of
(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})^{1/2}\mathbf{\Xi}\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})^{1/2}
. Then it is straightforward to see that
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})^{-1/2}\mathbf{V}
since they both have orthonormal columns. It suffices to argue that
\mathbf{V}
does not have repeated rows. Assuming this for the moment, by taking
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})\mathbf{V}
, it holds that
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{M}
, with
\mathbf{M}
having no repeated rows, whence the result is proven.

It remains to argue that
\mathbf{V}
does not have repeated rows. Under the conditions of
Theorem

2.1
we have already shown that
\mathbf{\Xi}
does not have repeated rows. Hence
\mathbf{\Xi}\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}
is a block matrix with no repeated rows and columns, and hence
(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})^{1/2}\mathbf{\Xi}\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})^{1/2}
is also a block matrix with no repeated rows and columns. Now assume for contradiction that
\mathbf{V}
has repeated rows. This implies that
\mathbf{V}=\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
for some matrices
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\in\{0,1\}^{K\times\tilde{K}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{K}\times\tilde{K}}
a full rank matrix. Suppose that
\mathbf{V}
has rows
r
and
r^{\prime}
repeated, and without loss of generality suppose that row is the first row of
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
(or else permute
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
), so that
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{r1}=\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{r^{\prime}1}=1
. Then from the equation
(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})^{1/2}\mathbf{\Xi}\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})^{1/2}=\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\Gamma\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}^{\top}
, it holds that for all
1\leq s\leq K
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{r}n_{s}}}\langle\xi_{r\cdot},\xi_{s\cdot}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\big(\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\Gamma^{1/2}\big)_{r\cdot},\big(\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\Gamma^{1/2}\big)_{s\cdot}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\big(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\Gamma^{1/2}\big)_{1\cdot},\big(\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\Gamma^{1/2}\big)_{s\cdot}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\big(\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\Gamma^{1/2}\big)_{r^{\prime}\cdot},\big(\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\Gamma^{1/2}\big)_{s\cdot}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{r^{\prime}}n_{s}}}\langle\xi_{r^{\prime}\cdot},\xi_{s\cdot}\rangle.

Consequently, since the above identity holds for all
s
, this shows that the
r
and
r^{\prime}
’th rows and columns of
(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})^{1/2}\mathbf{\Xi}\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})^{1/2}
are identical. However, this is a contradiction, which completes the proof.
∎

B.3
Proof of Lemma
A.1

We will restate
Lemma

A.1
for convenience.

See
A.1

Proof of
Lemma

A.1
.

Define the matrix

\mathbf{G}^{(l)}:=K\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{-2}\diag(\|\theta_{\mathcal{C}(1)}^{(l)}\|,\dots,\|\theta_{\mathcal{C}(K)}^{(l)}\|)\mathbf{B}^{(l)}\diag(\|\theta_{\mathcal{C}(1)}^{(l)}\|,\dots,\|\theta_{\mathcal{C}(K)}^{(l)}\|).

Letting
\lambda_{r}(\cdot)
denote the eigenvalues of a matrix, by Ostrowski’s Theorem (Theorem 4.5.9 of
Horn and Johnson 2012
) and
1
, the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{G}^{(l)}
satisfy
\lambda_{r}(\mathbf{G}^{(l)})\asymp\lambda_{r}(\mathbf{B}^{(l)})
. Since the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{P}^{(l)}=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{B}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}
are the same as the eigenvalues of the matrix

\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\mathbf{B}^{(l)}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2},

we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}\bigg(\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\mathbf{B}^{(l)}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\bigg)\asymp\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}{K}\lambda_{r}\big(\mathbf{G}^{(l)}\big)\asymp\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}{K}\lambda_{r}(\mathbf{B}^{(l)}).

To prove the other two assertions, we first observe that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{-1/2}\bigg[\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\mathbf{B}^{(l)}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\bigg]\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{-1/2}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}.

Suppose that the matrix
\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\mathbf{B}^{(l)}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}
has eigendecomposition
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\Lambda^{(l)}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}
, which is permissible as both matrices share the same eigenvalues.
Then it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{(l)}=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{-1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\Lambda^{(l)}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{-1/2}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}.

However, since the columns of the matrix
\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{-1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}
are orthonormal, the decomposition above is a valid
eigenvector-eigenvalue decomposition for
\mathbf{P}^{(l)}
. In particular, this shows that without loss of generality, we may take
\mathbf{U}^{(l)}
to be

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{(l)}=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{-1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}.

We immediately obtain the bound

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{U}_{i\cdot}^{(l)}\|

\displaystyle=\theta_{i}^{(l)}\frac{1}{\|\theta^{(l)}_{\mathcal{C}(z(i))}\|}\lesssim\theta_{i}^{(l)}\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|},

where we have used the fact that
\|\theta_{\mathcal{C}(r)}\|^{2}\asymp\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}{K}
for all
r
. Similarly, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot}^{(l)}\|\leq\|\mathbf{U}^{(l)}_{i\cdot}\|\||\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2}\|\lesssim\theta_{i}^{(l)}{\sqrt{K}}.

It remains to provide a lower bound on
\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot}
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{-1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2}.

Observe that
\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{-1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}=\mathbf{B}^{(l)}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}(\Lambda^{(l)})^{-1}
, which shows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{B}^{(l)}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}(\Lambda^{(l)})^{-1}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{B}^{(l)}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{W},

where
\mathbf{W}
is the diagonal matrix of signs of
\Lambda^{(l)}
.
Consider a given row
i
and suppose that
z(i)=r
. Then by Ostrowki’s Theorem again,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{X}^{(l)}_{i\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\geq\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\mathbf{B}^{(l)}_{r\cdot}\|\sigma_{\min}\bigg(\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{W}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\mathbf{B}^{(l)}_{r\cdot}\|\sigma_{\min}\big(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}\big)^{1/2}\sigma_{\min}\big(|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\mathbf{B}^{(l)}_{r\cdot}\|\min_{r}\|\theta_{\mathcal{C}(r)}\|\sigma_{\min}\big(|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\mathbf{B}^{(l)}_{r\cdot}\|\min_{r}\|\theta^{(l)}_{\mathcal{C}(r)}\|\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\theta_{i}^{(l)}\|\mathbf{B}^{(l)}_{r\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\gtrsim\theta_{i}^{(l)},

where the final line follows from the assumption that
\mathbf{B}^{(l)}
has unit diagonals. This completes the proof.
∎

B.4
Proof of Lemma
A.2

We restate
Lemma

A.2
for convenience.

See
A.2

Proof of
Lemma

A.2
.

The first part of the proof holds by Lemma 2.1 of
Lei and Rinaldo 2015
applied to the matrix
\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}
, which is a block matrix. See also the proof of
Proposition

2.1
.

For the second part we proceed as follows. First recall by the proof of
Proposition

2.1
that we can write the matrix
\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}
as the matrix

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\mathbf{\Xi}^{(l)}(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(l)})^{\top},

where the matrix
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(l)}
is defined as follows. First, let
\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}
be the matrix such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{(l)}=\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}.

Then the rows of
\Xi^{(l)}
are equal to the rows of
\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2}
normalized by their magnitude. It was discussed in the proof of
Proposition

2.1
that the entries of
\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}
are of order
\frac{1}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|}
. Observe that we can write
\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}=\mathbf{Z}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2}
, where
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)}
is the
K\times K
diagonal matrix of row norms of
\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2}
. Observe that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\max_{i}\|\big(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{1/2}\big)_{i\cdot}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\max_{i}\sum_{r=1}^{K}(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}_{ir})^{2}|\lambda_{r}|

\displaystyle=\max_{i}\sum_{r=2}^{K}\frac{C}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}{K}|\lambda_{r}(\mathbf{B}^{(l)})|+\frac{C}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

where we have applied
Lemma

A.1
to observe that
\lambda_{r}\asymp\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}{K}\lambda_{r}(\mathbf{B}^{(l)})
for
2\leq r\leq K
and
\lambda_{1}\asymp\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}
, since by
1
that the largest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{B}^{(l)}
is upper bounded by
CK
. Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}\bigg(\sum_{l}\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\min}\bigg(\sum_{l}\mathbf{Z}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\min}\bigg(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}\big(\sum_{l}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})\lambda_{\min}\bigg(\sum_{l}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\bigg)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{n}{K}L\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}\lambda_{\min}\bigg[(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\bigg]\bigg)

where we have used the fact that
\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})=n_{\min}\asymp n/K
and that the term inside the sum is rank
K
and hence invertible. Consequently, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}\bigg[(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\bigg]\gtrsim\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}.

However, by the argument in
Lemma

A.1
, it holds that
\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}=(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z})^{-1}
.
Set
\mathbf{G}^{(l)}:=K^{-1}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)})^{2}\mathbf{Z}).
By
1
,
\|(\mathbf{G}^{(l)})^{-1}\|\leq C
and
\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}=K\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{-2}(\mathbf{G}^{(l)})^{-1}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}\bigg[(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1}\bigg]

\displaystyle\gtrsim\lambda_{\min}((\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(l)})^{-1})^{2}\lambda_{\min}\big(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|(\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}\big)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\lambda_{\min}\big((\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|\big)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\lambda_{\min}\big((\mathbf{Q}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{Q}^{(l)}\big)\lambda_{\min}(|\Lambda^{(l)}|)

\displaystyle\gtrsim K\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{-2}\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{(l)})\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}{K}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\lambda_{\min}^{(l)},

where we have used
1
and
Lemma

A.1
implicitly.
This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of First Stage Characterization (Theorem
A.1
)

This section contains the full proof of
Theorem

A.1
. First, we will restate
Theorem

A.1
here for convenience.

See
A.1

As an immediate application of
Theorem

A.1
, we can obtain a spectral norm concentration bound for the residual, which will be useful in subsequent steps.

Lemma C.1
.

The residual term
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ I}}^{(l)}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ I}}^{(l)}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{n}\frac{K^{2}\theta_{\max}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}}\bigg(\log(n)\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}}+\frac{{\sqrt{K}}}{\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}+\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{K^{5/2}\log(n)}{(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\bigg).

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-15})
.

The proof of this result follows immediately by noting that
\|\cdot\|\leq\sqrt{n}\|\cdot\|_{2,\infty}
and the bound in
Theorem

A.1
.

We will also use an
\ell_{2,\infty}
bound for the linear term appearing in
Theorem

A.1
in the proof of
Theorem

A.3
.

Lemma C.2
.

The linear term in
Theorem

3.1
satisfies, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-15}),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{K\sqrt{\log(n)}}{(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}\|\theta^{(l)}\|}.

Proof of
Lemma

C.2
.

Throughout this proof we suppress the dependence of
\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l)},\Lambda^{(l)}
and
\mathbf{U}^{(l)}
on the index
l
, and we denote
\lambda
via
\frac{1}{\lambda}=\|(\Lambda^{(l)})^{-1}\|.
Define
\mathbf{E}:=\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)}
, so that
\mathbf{E}
is a mean-zero random matrix.

We will apply the Matrix Bernstein inequality to each row separately. To wit, by Corollary 3.3 of
Chen et al. 2021b
, we have that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-16})
it holds that

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{v\log(n)}+w\log(n),

where

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\max\bigg\{\bigg\|\sum_{j}\bigg(\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\bigg)_{j\cdot}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{E}_{ij}^{2}\bigg(\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\bigg)_{j\cdot}\bigg\|,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\bigg\|\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{E}_{ij}\bigg(\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\bigg)_{j\cdot}\bigg(\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\bigg)_{j\cdot}^{\top}\mathbf{E}_{ij}\bigg\|\bigg\};

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=\max_{j}\bigg\|\mathbf{E}_{ij}\bigg(\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\bigg)_{j\cdot}\bigg\|.

Since
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{E}_{ij}^{2})
is a scalar, we have that by
Lemma

A.1
,

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{E}_{ij}^{2}\bigg\|\bigg(\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\bigg)_{j\cdot}\bigg\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j}\theta_{i}\theta_{j}\|e_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|^{2}\frac{1}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j}\theta_{i}\theta_{j}\frac{\theta_{j}^{2}K}{\|\theta\|^{2}}\frac{K}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}}\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot}\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j}\theta_{i}\theta_{j}\frac{\theta_{j}^{2}K}{\|\theta\|^{2}}\frac{K}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}}\frac{1}{\theta_{i}^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j}\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}\|\theta\|^{4}}\bigg(\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta_{i}}\bigg)\theta_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}\|\theta\|^{4}}\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)\|\theta\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}\|\theta\|^{2}}\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg).

Similarly,

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j}\bigg\|\bigg(\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\bigg)_{j\cdot}\bigg\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\theta_{\max}\sqrt{K}}{\|\theta\|}\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\|\theta\|\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}}\frac{1}{\theta_{i}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)\frac{K}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}}.

Therefore, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-16})
, we have that

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{K\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}\|\theta\|}+\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)\frac{K\log(n)}{\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}\|\theta\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{K\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}\|\theta\|}\max\bigg\{1,\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\|\theta\|}\bigg\}.

We now show that
2
implies that 1 is the maximum above.
2
states that

\displaystyle C\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\frac{K^{8}\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}{\|\theta\|^{4}(\lambda_{\min})^{2}}\leq\bar{\lambda}.

Since
\bar{\lambda}\leq 1
by assumption and
\|\theta\|^{2}\leq\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}
, it is straightforward to verify that
2
implies that

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)\frac{\log(n)}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}\lesssim 1.

Taking square roots reveals that

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|}\lesssim 1,

which shows that one is the dominant term in the maximum, as long as
C
is larger than some universal constant.
Taking a union bound over all the rows completes the proof.
∎

C.1
Preliminary Lemmas

Throughout this section and its proof we suppress the dependence on
l
in all terms. We also let
\lambda
denote the absolute value of the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of
\mathbf{P}
. In what follows, we will assume that
\lambda\gtrsim\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}
, which by
Lemma

A.1
holds under
2
. We will verify this explicitly at the beginning of the proof of
Theorem

A.1
.

The following result shows a form of spectral norm concentration.

Lemma C.3

(Spectral Norm Concentration for One Graph)
.

When
\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\geq\log(n)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}};

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)},

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20})
.

Proof.

See Lemma C.1 of
Jin et al. 2024
, or directly apply Remark 3.13 from
Bandeira and Handel 2016
.
The other part follows from a straightforward
\varepsilon
-net argument.
∎

The following lemma demonstrates good concentration for several residual terms, showing that several terms “approximately commute.”

Lemma C.4

(Approximate Commutation)
.

When
\lambda\gtrsim\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}
and
\min_{i}\theta_{i}\|\theta\|_{1}\gtrsim\log(n)
, the following bounds hold with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20}):

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda^{2}}

(C.1)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{1/2}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda^{1/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg)

(C.2)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda^{3/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg).

(C.3)

Proof of
Lemma

C.4
.

For (
C.1
), the argument follows since
\mathbf{W}_{*}
is the product of the orthogonal matrices in the singular value decomposition of
\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}
and hence

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{I}-\cos\mathbf{\Theta}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{\widehat{U}})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda^{2}}

which holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20})
by
Lemma

C.3
.

For all the following terms, we first show that
|\widehat{\Lambda}|(\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q})
is sufficiently small by modifying a similar argument to
Rubin-Delanchy et al. 2022
. Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&2\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{+}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{-}\\
-2\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{+}&0\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathbf{U}_{+}
denotes the eigenvectors of
\mathbf{U}
corresponding to the positive eigenvectors (and similarly for
\mathbf{U}_{-}
,
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{+}
, and
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}
respectively). Let
\mathbf{u}_{j}^{+}
and
\mathbf{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{-}
denote the
j
’th columns of
\mathbf{U}_{+}
and
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}
respectively. Then the
j,i
entry of
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{+}
is simply
(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{+})^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{-}
, and hence by the eigenvector-eigenvalue equation,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{+})^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{-}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{+})^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{\widehat{u}}_{j,-}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j,-}-\lambda_{i,+}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{+})^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}_{-}\mathbf{U}_{-}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{u}}_{j,-}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j,-}-\lambda_{i,+}}+\frac{(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{+})^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\big(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}_{-}\mathbf{U}_{-}^{\top}\big)\mathbf{\widehat{u}}_{j,-}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j,-}-\lambda_{i,+}},

where
\lambda_{i,+}
denotes the
i
’th largest in magnitude eigenvalue of
\mathbf{P}
(and similarly for
\widehat{\lambda}_{j,-}
for the negative eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}
). It is straightforward to check that the
j,i
entry of the matrix
|\widehat{\Lambda}_{+}|(\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{+}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{-}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{+}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{-}\mathbf{I}_{p,q})
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{|\widehat{\lambda}_{j+}|}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j,+}-\lambda_{i,-}}(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{+})^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{\widehat{u}}_{j,-}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\frac{\lambda_{i,-}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j,+}}}(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{+})^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}_{+}\mathbf{U}_{+}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{u}}_{j,-}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{1-\frac{\lambda_{i,-}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j,+}}}(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{+})^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}_{+}\mathbf{U}_{+}^{\top})\mathbf{\widehat{u}}_{j,-}

Since
\lambda_{i,-}
is negative and
\widehat{\lambda}_{j,+}
is positive with high probability,
1-\frac{\lambda_{i,-}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j,+}}
is strictly larger than one. A similar argument holds for the entries with the “
+
” changed to a “
-
”.

Without loss of generality, consider the term corresponding to the negative eigenvalues.
We can write the matrix as follows. Denote
\mathbf{M}
as the matrix whose
i,j
entry is
\frac{|\widehat{\lambda}_{j,-}|}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j,-}-\lambda_{i,+}}
. Then we have the equality

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{+}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}|\widehat{\Lambda}_{-}|

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}\circ\bigg(\mathbf{U}_{+}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}_{-}\mathbf{U}_{-}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}+\mathbf{U}_{+}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\big(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}_{-}\mathbf{U}_{-}^{\top}\big)\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}\bigg).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{U}_{+}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}|\widehat{\Lambda}_{-}|\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{M}\|\bigg(\|\mathbf{U}_{+}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}_{-}\|+\|\mathbf{U}_{+}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\big(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}_{-}\mathbf{U}_{-}^{\top}\big)\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}\|\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{M}\|\bigg(\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}\|+\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|\|\big(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}_{-}\mathbf{U}_{-}^{\top}\big)\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}\|\bigg),

(C.4)

where we have used the fact that
\mathbf{U}_{+}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}_{-}
is a submatrix of
\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}
. We now note that

\displaystyle\|\big(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}_{-}\mathbf{U}_{-}^{\top}\big)\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}\|

\displaystyle=\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{U}_{-},\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-})\|.

In addition, the eigenvalues corresponding to
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}
are all negative, and the eigengap condition is satisfied since the eigenvalues corresponding to
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}_{-}\mathbf{U}_{-})\mathbf{P}
are either all zero or positive. Consequently, the eigengap satisfies

\displaystyle\min_{\lambda_{i}>0}\lambda_{i}-\max_{p+1\leq i\leq n}\widehat{\lambda}_{i}\gtrsim\lambda

by applying Weyl’s inequality to the negative eigenvalues and the bottom
n-K
eigenvalues separately. We can therefore apply the Davis-Kahan Theorem to obtain

\displaystyle\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{U}_{-},\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}}{\lambda}

(C.5)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20})
. In addition, observe that the matrix
\mathbf{M}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{M}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim K.

(C.6)

Finally, by
Lemma

C.3
, we have that
\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}\|\lesssim\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}
with high probability. Plugging in this estimate, (
C.6
), and (
C.5
) into (
C.4
) yields

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{U}_{+}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim K\bigg(\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg).

Therefore, by applying a similar argument to
\mathbf{U}_{-}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{+}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\||\widehat{\Lambda}|(\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim K\bigg(\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}\|+\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{U}_{-},\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{-})\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{U}_{+},\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{+})\|\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim K\bigg(\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg),

(C.7)

which holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20})
.

We now bound (
C.2
). First, note that we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{1/2}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\|

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{1/2}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\|,

where the first line follows since
\mathbf{I}_{p,q}
is orthogonal and the second line follows since diagonal matrices commute. We observe that the
k,l
entry of the matrix above can be written as

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{1/2}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\bigg)_{kl}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{\cdot k},\mathbf{U}_{\cdot l}(\mathbf{I}_{p,q})_{ll}\rangle\bigg(|\lambda_{l}|^{1/2}-|\widehat{\lambda}_{k}|^{1/2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{\cdot k},\mathbf{U}_{\cdot l}\rangle(\mathbf{I}_{p,q})_{ll}\frac{|\lambda_{l}|-|\widehat{\lambda}_{k}|}{|\lambda_{l}|^{1/2}+|\widehat{\lambda}_{k}|^{1/2}}.

Define the matrix
\mathbf{H}
via
\mathbf{H}_{kl}:=\frac{1}{|\lambda_{l}|^{1/2}+|\widehat{\lambda}_{k}|^{1/2}}
. Then the matrix
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{1/2}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{1/2}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}\circ\bigg(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|-|\widehat{\Lambda}|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}\circ\bigg(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|-|\widehat{\Lambda}|\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbf{H}\circ\bigg(|\widehat{\Lambda}|\bigg(\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}\circ\bigg(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Lambda-\widehat{\Lambda}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbf{H}\circ\bigg(|\widehat{\Lambda}|\bigg(\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\bigg)\bigg).

where
\circ
denotes the Hadamard product. It is straightforward to observe that
\|\mathbf{H}\|\lesssim\frac{K}{\lambda^{1/2}}
. Consequently, we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{1/2}

\displaystyle-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{H}\|\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Lambda-\widehat{\Lambda}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|+\|\mathbf{H}\|\||\widehat{\Lambda}|\big(\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\big)\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K}{\lambda^{1/2}}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Lambda-\widehat{\Lambda}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|+\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda^{1/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K}{\lambda^{1/2}}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}\|+\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda^{1/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K}{\lambda^{1/2}}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}(\mathbf{P}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{U}\|+\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda^{1/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg).

(C.8)

We note that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}(\mathbf{P}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{U}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{P}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{U}\|+\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})(\mathbf{P}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{U}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}.

Plugging this into our bound (
C.8
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{1/2}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K}{\lambda^{1/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda^{1/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda^{1/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg).

This proves (
C.2
).

We now consider the term (
C.3
). Since diagonal matrices commute,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}

\displaystyle-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|

\displaystyle=\||\widehat{\Lambda}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\bigg(|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{1/2}\bigg)\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\||\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{1/2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\bigg(\||\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}(\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q})\|+\||\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{1/2}\|\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda^{3/2}}\||\widehat{\Lambda}|(\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q})\|+\frac{1}{\lambda}\||\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}|\Lambda|^{1/2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K}{\lambda^{3/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda^{3/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\lambda^{3/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg).

where we have implicitly used the bound (
C.2
) and (
C.8
).
This bound holds cumulatively with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20})
, which completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma characterizes the row-wise concentration of terms that involve
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}
. However, this proof requires the use of leave-one-out sequences, so we defer its proof to
Section

C.3
after the proof of
Theorem

A.1
.

Lemma C.5

(Row-Wise Concentration I)
.

When
\lambda\gtrsim\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}
and
\min_{i}\theta_{i}\|\theta\|_{1}\gtrsim\log(n)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{A})\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\theta_{i}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}

The following result demonstrates that
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}
is sufficiently close to
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}
in
\|\cdot\|_{2,\infty}
.

Lemma C.6

(Closeness of
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}
to
\mathbf{U}
)
.

When
\lambda\gtrsim\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}
and
\min_{i}\theta_{i}\|\theta\|_{1}\gtrsim\log(n)
, the following bounds holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-19}):

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}_{*}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}.

The bound above matches the bound in
Jin et al. 2024
, Lemma D.2.

Proof of
Lemma

C.6
.

Observe that since
\mathbf{U}
are the eigenvectors of
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{P}
is rank
K
,

\displaystyle e_{i}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\big)

\displaystyle=e_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathbf{\widehat{U}}

\displaystyle=e_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-1}

\displaystyle=e_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-1}

\displaystyle=e_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-1}-e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-1}.

Taking norms reveals that

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\big)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|e_{i}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\big)\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|\|\widehat{\Lambda}^{-1}\|+\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|\|\widehat{\Lambda}^{-1}\|.

By
Lemma

C.3
, we have that
\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|\lesssim\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}
. In addition, Weyl’s inequality implies that
\|\widehat{\Lambda}^{-1}\|\lesssim\lambda^{-1}
. Therefore, combining these bounds with
Lemma

C.5
, we see that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20})
that

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\big)\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{i}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}+\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}+\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}}{\lambda}.

This bound is independent of row
i
, so taking a union bound reveals that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-19})
that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}+\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}}{\lambda}.

By
Lemma

C.4
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda^{2}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}_{*}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}+\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\|\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}+\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}}{\lambda}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda^{2}}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}+\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}}{\lambda}.

As a byproduct, this also reveals that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}_{*}\|_{2,\infty}+\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}+\frac{3}{2}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty},

as long as
\lambda\geq C\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}
for some sufficiently large constant
C
(which we verify at the beginning of the proof of
Theorem

A.1
, and which holds under
2
). By rearranging, it holds that
\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}
. Plugging this in yields

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}_{*}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty};

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}.

The final inequality holds since

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}+\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}_{*}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|_{2,\infty}\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}\|+\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}_{*}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\|\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|+\|\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}_{*}\|_{2,\infty}.

The proof is completed by plugging in the previous bounds.
∎

The following result establishes finer control over the rows of the estimated eigenvectors. We relegate the proof of his result to
Section

C.3
, since it requires the use of leave-one-out sequences.

Lemma C.7

(Row-wise Concentration II)
.

When
\lambda\gtrsim\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}
and
\min_{i}\theta_{i}\|\theta\|_{1}\gtrsim\log(n)
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-19})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{A})(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{U})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\theta_{i}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}\|\frac{\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\lambda}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}.

C.2
Proof of Theorem
A.1

Proof of
Theorem

A.1
.

Throughout the proof we suppress the dependence of these terms on the index
l
. Our proof proceeds in several steps: first, we express
\mathbf{\widehat{X}}\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{X}
as a linear term plus a residual term, where the residual term obeys a strong row-wise concentration bound. Next, we demonstrate that the rows of
\mathbf{\widehat{Y}}
(i.e. the normalized rows of
\mathbf{\widehat{X}}
) concentrate about the corresponding rows of
\mathbf{Y}
. Before embarking on the proof, we make note of several preliminary facts. By
Lemma

A.1
, we have that

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}}{K};

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{K}\theta_{i}}{\|\theta\|};

\displaystyle\theta_{i}\lesssim\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|

\displaystyle\leq\theta_{i}{\sqrt{K}}.

We will use these bounds repeatedly without reference when simplifying our results.

In addition, many of the previous lemmas require that
\lambda\gtrsim\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}
. We verify that this condition holds under
2
now.

2
requires that

\displaystyle C\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)\frac{K^{8}\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}{\|\theta\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{2}}\leq\bar{\lambda}.

(C.9)

By
Lemma

A.1
it holds that

\displaystyle\lambda\gtrsim\frac{\|\theta\|^{2}}{K}\lambda_{\min}.

Consequently, it suffices to argue that (
C.9
) implies the condition

\displaystyle\frac{\|\theta\|^{2}}{K}\lambda_{\min}\gtrsim\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)},

or equivalently,

\displaystyle\frac{K\sqrt{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}}\lesssim 1.

Squaring both sides yields the condition

\displaystyle\frac{K^{2}\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}{\|\theta\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{2}}\lesssim 1.

This is weaker than (
C.9
) as
\lambda_{\min},\bar{\lambda}\in(0,1)
by assumption and
K\geq 1
, as long as
C
is larger than some universal constant.

Step 1: First-Order Approximation of
\mathbf{\widehat{X}}
:

At the outset we recall that
\mathbf{W}_{*}
is the Frobenius-optimal matrix aligning
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}
and
\mathbf{U}
. Moreover, by the concentration inequality in
Lemma

C.3
and the assumption on the eigenvalue
\lambda
above, we have that
\|\widehat{\Lambda}^{-1}\|\lesssim\lambda^{-1}
with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20})
. We now expand via:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\widehat{X}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{X}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{A}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{A})\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}+\mathbf{R};

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{1}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}+\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}+\mathbf{R}_{3}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}+\mathbf{R}_{4}+\mathbf{R}_{5}+\mathbf{R}_{6};

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{1}:

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{A})\mathbf{\widehat{U}}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q};

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{2}:

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}-|\Lambda|^{1/2}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}});

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{3}:

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{1/2}(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{W}_{*});

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{4}:

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{A}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{A})(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{U})|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q};

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{5}:

\displaystyle=-(\mathbf{A}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{A})\mathbf{\widehat{U}}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}-|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U});

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{6}:

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{A}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{A})\mathbf{\widehat{U}}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}(\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}).

We now bound each residual in turn. We will also use
Lemma

C.4
,
Lemma

C.5
,
Lemma

C.6
,
Lemma

C.7
repeatedly without reference; the cumulative probability will be at least
1-O(n^{-18})
.

The term
\mathbf{R}_{1}
:

First, we note that

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{1}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{\widehat{U}}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{-1/2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|}{\lambda^{1/2}}\bigg(\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}\|+\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|\|\mathbf{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|}{\lambda^{1/2}}\bigg(\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}\|+\frac{\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}\|^{2}}{\lambda}\bigg).

By
Lemma

C.3
, we have that
\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P})\mathbf{U}\|\lesssim\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}
with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20})
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{1}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|}{\lambda^{1/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\lambda}\bigg).

By
Lemma

A.1
, we have that
\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{K}\theta_{i}}{\|\theta\|}
and that
\lambda\gtrsim\frac{\|\theta\|^{2}}{K}\lambda_{\min}
. Putting it together, we arrive at the bound

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{1}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K\theta_{i}}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{K\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K\theta_{i}}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}}\bigg(\sqrt{K\log(n)}+\frac{K\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}}\bigg).

(C.10)

The term
\mathbf{R}_{2}
:

We have

\displaystyle\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{2}\|
