\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{all}}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2};

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{6}:

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{W}_{*})\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}.

By
Lemma

E.1
, we have the bounds

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{4}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{n\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2};

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{5}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3/2}\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K^{5/2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{n\bar{\lambda}}}.

In addition, by properties of the
\ell_{2,\infty}
norm and
Lemma

E.2
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{6}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{W}_{*}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}}\bigg(K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}+K^{3}\log(n)\frac{\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{9/2}\log(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{\sqrt{n}L\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{5/2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K^{3/2}\alpha_{\max}^{2}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}},

where we have used the fact that each of the terms inside of the parentheses on the bound for
\mathbf{R}_{6}
is less than one, which was verified in the proof of
Theorem

A.2
(note that these terms in parentheses are simply the
\sin\mathbf{\Theta}
upper bound).

Combining these, we obtain that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{4}\|_{2,\infty}+\|\mathbf{R}_{5}\|_{2,\infty}+\|\mathbf{R}_{6}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{n\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{\sqrt{n}L\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K^{5/2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}},

where we have used the fact that
\frac{K^{3/2}\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}\lesssim 1
.

For the terms
\mathbf{R}_{1}
through
\mathbf{R}_{3}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{1}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K}{nL\bar{\lambda}}\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}\|\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|;

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{2}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}\|\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}\|;

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{3}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K}{nL\bar{\lambda}}\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|.

Lemma

E.2
shows that
with probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{W}_{*}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}+K^{3}\log(n)\frac{\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}\bigg)^{2};

\displaystyle\|\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{nL^{3/2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{2}\alpha_{\max}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}};

\displaystyle\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim K\sqrt{Ln\log(n)}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}.

In addition, by
Theorem

A.2
, we have that

\displaystyle\|\sin\mathbf{\Theta}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}+K^{3}\log(n)\frac{\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\quad+K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}.

Plugging these bounds in yields that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{1}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K}{nL\bar{\lambda}}K\sqrt{Ln\log(n)}(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg(K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}+K^{3}\log(n)\frac{\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{L\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{5}\log^{3/2}(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}};

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{2}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim K\sqrt{Ln\log(n)}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg(\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{nL^{3/2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{2}\alpha_{\max}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{\sqrt{n}L\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{5}\log^{3/2}(n)\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}};

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{3}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K}{nL\bar{\lambda}}K\sqrt{Ln\log(n)}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\times\bigg(K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}+K^{3}\log(n)\frac{\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\quad+K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{L\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{5}\log^{3/2}(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}.

We note that we have used the fact that

\displaystyle K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}+K^{3}\log(n)\frac{\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|^{2}_{\infty}}{\bar{\lambda}}+K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}\lesssim 1,

as was verified in the proof of
Theorem

A.2
(observe that this term matches the
\sin\mathbf{\Theta}
upper bound, and hence is less than one by assumption). Consequently, since each term is the same, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{1}\|_{2,\infty}+

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}_{2}\|_{2,\infty}+\|\mathbf{R}_{3}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{L\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{5}\log^{3/2}(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{L\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{5}\log^{3/2}(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}},

where we have used the assumption that that
K\sqrt{\log(n)}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}\lesssim 1
, which follows immediately the fact that
\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}\geq 1
and from
2
, which requires that
K^{8}\log(n)\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}
. Therefore, we have shown that

\displaystyle\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{U}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}+\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}},

with

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{n\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{\sqrt{n}L\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K^{5/2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{L\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{5}\log^{3/2}(n)\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{nL\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)}{L^{2}\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{5/2}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}+\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{5}\log^{3/2}(n)}{L\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{L\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{nL\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)}{L^{2}\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}\bigg(1+\frac{K^{3/2}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{L\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}\bigg(1+K^{5/2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{L\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{nL\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)}{L^{2}\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}+\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}},

where the final inequality holds as long as

\displaystyle\frac{K^{3/2}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{L\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1;

(E.6)

\displaystyle K^{5/2}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1

(E.7)

\displaystyle\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{L\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1.

(E.8)

We will verify these bounds now. First,
2
implies that

\displaystyle\frac{K^{8}\log(n)\theta_{\max}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{2}^{2}}(\mathrm{SNR}_{l}^{-1})^{2}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)},

as long as
C
in the assumption is sufficiently large.
Observe that this immediately implies equation (
E.7
) since
\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{2}^{2}}\geq 1
and
\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}\in(0,1)
by assumption. For the other two terms, by averaging this condition over
l
, we see that
2
implies

\displaystyle\frac{K^{8}\log(n)}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}^{2}.

(E.9)

This implies (
E.6
) and (
E.8
).
Hence, we have shown so far that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{nL\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)}{L^{2}\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}+\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}.

This holds cumulatively with probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
. We now verify that the sum of these terms is less than
\frac{1}{16\sqrt{n_{\max}}}
. Since
n_{\max}\leq n
, it suffices to show that this upper bound is at most
\frac{1}{16\sqrt{n}}
. By pulling out a factor of
1/\sqrt{n}
it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\sqrt{n}L\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)}{L^{2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{K^{7/2}\log(n)}{\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}+\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}\lesssim 1.

By similar manipulations as in verifying the bounds (
E.6
), (
E.7
), and (
E.8
), it is straightforward to check the condition above holds, except for the condition
\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}\lesssim 1
. Plugging in the definition for
\alpha_{\max}
, we see that we require

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\bar{\lambda}}\max_{l}\frac{K^{2}\theta_{\max}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}}\bigg(\log(n)\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}}+\frac{{\sqrt{K}}}{\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}+\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{K^{5/2}\log(n)}{(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\bigg)\lesssim 1.

This is covered by
2
. Therefore, this completes the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Proof of Extension to Different Network Setting (Theorem
3.3
)

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
be the same as the matrix
\mathcal{Y}
, except constructed using the “true” membership matrix
\mathbf{Z}
. First we study the spectral structure of
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, yielding a
\sin\Theta
bound between their respective eigenvectors. We then use this result together with a deterministic
\ell_{2,\infty}
bound from
Cape et al. 2019
bound the
\ell_{2,\infty}
difference. Combining these results we provide a modified proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

Step 1: Spectral Structure of
\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}
. First, we note that
Lemma

A.2
applies to
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}
, and hence it holds that

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}_{Y}\gtrsim\frac{n}{K}L\bar{\lambda}.

Furthermore, since the first phase population analysis continues to hold without modification for
\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}
, it holds that
\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}=\sum_{l}\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}\mathbf{M}^{(l)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(l)})^{\top}
for some (positive definite) matrices
\mathbf{M}^{(l)}
. The proof of this same lemma reveals further that
\max_{l}\|\mathbf{M}^{(l)}\|\lesssim 1
. We also have that
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbf{Z}\big(\sum_{l}\mathbf{M}^{(l)}\big)\mathbf{Z}^{\top}
. Therefore, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}-\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}\|

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\sum_{l}\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}\mathbf{M}^{(l)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(l)})^{\top}-\mathbf{Z}\big(\sum_{l}\mathbf{M}^{(l)}\big)\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\sum_{l}\big[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}]\mathbf{M}^{(l)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(l)})^{\top}\bigg\|+\bigg\|\sum_{l}\big[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}]\mathbf{M}^{(l)}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\bigg\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\sum_{l}\big[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}\big]\mathbf{M}^{(l)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(l)})^{\top}\bigg\|+n\max_{i}\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\sum_{l}\big[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}\big]\mathbf{M}^{(l)}\bigg\|

\displaystyle\lesssim n\max_{i}\sum_{l}\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}\big]\bigg\|+n\max_{i}\sum_{l}\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}\big]\bigg\|

\displaystyle\lesssim nL\delta.

Therefore, under the assumption
\delta\ll\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{K}
, letting
\tilde{\lambda}^{2}_{Y}
denote the
K
-th eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}
, Weyl’s inequality implies that
\tilde{\lambda}^{2}_{Y}\gtrsim\frac{n}{K}L\bar{\lambda}
. Therefore, the Davis-Kahan Theorem implies that

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\mathbf{U},\tilde{\mathbf{U}})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim K\frac{\delta}{\bar{\lambda}}.

(F.1)

Note that under our assumptions the quantity on the right hand side above is
o(1)
.

Step 2: Bounding The
\ell_{2,\infty}
difference
. We apply Theorem 3.7 of
Cape et al. 2019
to reveal that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathbf{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}_{U}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\underbrace{\frac{\|(\mathbf{I}-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top})(\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}-\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top})\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}}{\lambda^{2}_{Y}}}_{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\frac{\|(\mathbf{I}-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top})(\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}-\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top})(\mathbf{I}-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top})\|_{2,\infty}}{\lambda^{2}_{Y}}\|\sin\Theta(\mathbf{U},\tilde{\mathbf{U}})\|}_{\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\|\sin\Theta(\mathbf{U},\tilde{\mathbf{U}})\|^{2}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}}_{\alpha_{3}}.

We will bound each term above separately. However, before doing so we observe that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}-\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\sum_{l}\big[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}]\mathbf{M}^{(l)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(l)})^{\top}\bigg\|+\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}\sum_{l}\mathbf{M}^{(l)}\big[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}]^{\top}\bigg\|.

For a fixed
i
it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\|e_{i}^{\top}

\displaystyle\big(\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}\big)\|\|\mathbf{M}^{(l)}\|\|\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}\|+\|\mathbf{Z}\|_{\infty,\infty}\max_{i}\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\sum_{l}\mathbf{M}^{(l)}[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}]^{\top}\bigg\|

\displaystyle\lesssim L\sqrt{n}\delta+\max_{i}\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\sum_{l}\mathbf{M}^{(l)}[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}]^{\top}\bigg\|

\displaystyle\lesssim L\sqrt{n}\delta+\max_{l}\|\mathbf{M}^{(l)}\|_{\max}\max_{i}\sum_{l}\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{Z}^{(l)}-\mathbf{Z}]\bigg\|

\displaystyle\lesssim L\sqrt{n}\delta.

(F.2)

We now bound
\alpha_{i}
in turn.

•

The term
\alpha_{1}
: By
Eq.

F.2
,

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{I}-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top})(\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}-\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top})\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\mathbf{I}-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top})\|_{\infty,\infty}\|\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}-\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{K}L\sqrt{n}\delta,

where
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\infty}
denotes the
\ell_{\infty}
operator norm on matrices, and the bound
\|(\mathbf{I}-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top})\|_{\infty,\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{K}
comes from the fact that
\|\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}}
. As a consequence, recalling that
\lambda^{2}_{Y}\gtrsim\frac{n}{K}L\bar{\lambda}
, we have that

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{K}L\sqrt{n}\delta}{\frac{n}{K}L\bar{\lambda}}\asymp\frac{K^{3/2}\delta}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}.

(F.3)

•

The term
\alpha_{2}
: By a similar argument as above,

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\sin\Theta(\mathbf{U},\tilde{\mathbf{U}})\|\frac{K^{3/2}\delta}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}\lesssim K^{5/2}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}^{2}\sqrt{n}},

(F.4)

where the final inequality follows from
Eq.

F.1
.

•

The term
\alpha_{3}
: By
Eq.

F.1
, we have that

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim K\frac{\delta}{\bar{\lambda}}\|\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim K^{3/2}\frac{\delta}{\bar{\lambda}\sqrt{n}}.

(F.5)

Therefore, combining
Eqs.

F.3
,
F.4
and
F.5
, we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{U}-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{W}_{U}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3/2}\delta}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}+K^{5/2}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}^{2}\sqrt{n}}+K^{3/2}\frac{\delta}{\bar{\lambda}\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3/2}\delta}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}},

where the final inequality follows from the assumption
\delta\ll\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{K}
. Note that this result also implies that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}}.

(F.6)

Step 3: Modifying the proof of
Theorem

3.1
:
First, the proof of
Theorem

A.1
goes through without modification since the proof only relies on the fact that each probability matrix is rank
K
, which continues to hold. The proof of
Theorem

A.2
also continues to hold without modification as it relies only on eigengap assumptions, which hold from the argument in Step 1 of this proof. As a result, the proof of
Theorem

A.3
nearly holds, except one now has the asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}_{*}=\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}+(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}+\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}},

where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ II}}
continues to have the same upper bound as before. In contrast to the case where all the
\mathbf{Z}
’s are the same, the additional quantity
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}
does not vanish as
\mathcal{Y}
is no longer exactly rank
K
, but instead only approximately so. The following lemma controls this additional term.

Lemma F.1
.

Under the conditions of
Theorem

3.3
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}\|_{2,\infty}\leq c_{0}\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}},

where
c_{0}
is some sufficiently small constant.

Explicitly, these results in tandem imply that

\displaystyle\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{\tilde{U}}\mathbf{W}_{U}\mathbf{W}_{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}+O\bigg(\frac{K^{3/2}\delta}{\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}\bigg)+c\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}+c\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}},

where the constant
c
is sufficiently small, where we have used the assumption
\delta\ll\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{K}
. We may therefore modify the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. First, we still have the bound
\|\sin\Theta(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\tilde{\mathbf{U}})\|\leq\frac{\beta}{8K\sqrt{C_{\varepsilon}}}
on the event
\mathcal{E}_{\sin\Theta}
. Replacing the appearances of
\mathbf{U}
with
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}
we see that step two of the argument remains valid without further modification, which relies only on the
\sin\Theta
bound. Step three also remains the same by appealing to the fact that
\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}}
by (
F.6
) (which is a deterministic bound). Therefore, with these modifications, the conclusion of
Theorem

3.1
continues to hold under the assumptions of
Theorem

3.3
, which completes the proof.

F.1
Proof of Lemma
F.1

Before proving this lemma, we introduce the following lemma establishing concentration inequalities for two terms that appear in the analysis.

Lemma F.2
.

With probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
it holds that

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\sqrt{L\log(n)};

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim Kn\sqrt{L\log(n)}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}.

Proof of
Lemma

F.2
.

The proof is similar to the lemmas in
Section

A.3
, where we write everything as a sum of independent random variables and apply Bernstein’s inequality. First, fix a row
i
of the matrix
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}
. Observe that

\displaystyle\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{K}\max_{1\leq\nu\leq K}\bigg|e_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}e_{\nu}\bigg|.

(F.7)

Let
\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp}
denote the
l
’th matrix
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}
. Then the
i,\nu
entry above can be written via

\displaystyle e_{i}^{\top}\sum_{l}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})\mathcal{L}\big(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)}\big)^{\top}\mathbf{U}e_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{c=1}^{n}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{ik}\mathcal{L}\big(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)}\big)_{ck}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f=1}^{n}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{ik}\bigg(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)}\bigg)_{cf}\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{fk}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f=1}^{n}\bigg(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)}\bigg)_{cf}\Bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{ik}\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{fk}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f\leq c,f=1}^{n}\bigg(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)}\bigg)_{cf}\Bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{ik}\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{fk}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{l}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f=c+1}^{n}\bigg(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)}\bigg)_{cf}\Bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{ik}\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{fk}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}\Bigg].

Observe that each term above is a sum over the independent random variables
\{(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)})_{cf}\}_{l=1}^{L}
. We bound the first term as the second term is similar. Bernstein’s inequality shows us that for fixed indices
i
and
\nu
it holds that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f\leq c,f=1}^{n}\bigg(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)}\bigg)_{cf}\Bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{ik}\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{fk}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{v\log(n)}+w\log(n),

where
v
is the sum of the variances and
w
is a bound on the maximum value for all
c,f
and
l
. We therefore bound directly, observing that by
Lemma

A.1
,

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f\leq cf=1}^{n}\theta^{(l)}_{c}\theta^{(l)}_{f}\Bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{ik}\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{fk}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}\Bigg]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f\leq cf=1}^{n}\theta^{(l)}_{c}\theta^{(l)}_{f}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}^{2}\Bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{ik}\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{fk}\Bigg]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f\leq cf=1}^{n}\theta^{(l)}_{c}\theta^{(l)}_{f}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}^{2}\|(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{i\cdot}\|^{2}\Bigg\|\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{f\cdot}\Bigg\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{l}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f=1}^{n}\theta^{(l)}_{c}\theta^{(l)}_{f}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}^{2}\|(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{i\cdot}\|^{2}\frac{K(\theta_{f}^{(l)})^{2}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}\frac{K}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}\frac{1}{(\theta_{f}^{(l)})^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim K^{2}\|(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{i\cdot}\|^{2}\sum_{l}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f=1}^{n}\theta^{(l)}_{c}\theta^{(l)}_{f}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}^{2}\frac{1}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}}{n}\|(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{i\cdot}\|^{2}L\max_{l}\frac{1}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f=1}^{n}\theta^{(l)}_{c}\theta^{(l)}_{f}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}}{n}\|(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{i\cdot}\|^{2}L\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}^{2}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}}{n}L\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}^{2}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}

(F.8)

where we have used the inequality (
F.6
) to bound
\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}^{2}\lesssim\frac{K}{n},
and the final inequality follows from the fact that
\|(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{i\cdot}\|\leq 1
. The maximum over
l,c,
and
f
is bounded by

\displaystyle\max_{l,c,f}\Bigg|\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{ik}\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{fk}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{l,f}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\|(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{i\cdot}\|\bigg\|\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{f\cdot}\bigg\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}}\frac{K}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{K^{3/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{l}\frac{1}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}

(F.9)

Therefore, combining (
F.8
) and (
F.9
), it holds that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-20})
,

\displaystyle\Bigg|\sum_{l}

\displaystyle\sum_{c=1}^{n}\sum_{f\leq c,f=1}^{n}\bigg(\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{P}^{(l)}\bigg)_{cf}\Bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\mathbf{Y}^{(l)}_{\perp})_{ik}\bigg(\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}^{(l)}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{f\cdot}\big)\bigg)_{fk}\mathbf{U}_{c\nu}\Bigg]\Bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{K^{3}}{n}L\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}^{2}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}\log(n)}+\frac{K^{3/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{l}\frac{1}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\log(n)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\sqrt{L\log(n)},

where the final inequality follows from the assumption that
\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}\gtrsim c\log(n)
. Therefore, combining this bound with the bound (
F.7
), we obtain the desired result.

We now bound the second quantity, though it is significantly easier due to the previous arguments. By
Lemma

C.4
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|e_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}\max_{1\leq\nu\leq n}\bigg|e_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}e_{\nu}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}\|\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})\mathcal{Y}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim Kn\sqrt{L\log(n)}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

F.1
.

First, we decompose via:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{W}_{*}}_{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}(\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}})}_{\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}(\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2})}_{\alpha_{3}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}})\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}}_{\alpha_{4}}.

We will bound each sequentially. Before moving on we note that by
Lemma

E.2
it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{W}_{*}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}+K^{3}\log(n)\frac{\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}^{2}

(F.10)

\displaystyle\|\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{nL^{3/2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{2}\alpha_{\max}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}};

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}

(F.11)

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}+K^{3}\log(n)\frac{\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\quad+K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\frac{\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{K\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}.

(F.12)

We now bound
\alpha_{i}
.

•

The term
\alpha_{1}
:
By
Lemma

F.2
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
,

\displaystyle\|\alpha_{1}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle=\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{W}_{*}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\|\Sigma^{-2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\sqrt{L\log(n)}\|\Sigma^{-2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\sqrt{L\log(n)}\frac{K}{nL\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{n^{3/2}\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}.

(F.13)

•

The term
\alpha_{2}
: Again by
Lemma

F.2
,

\displaystyle\|\alpha_{2}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle=\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\Sigma^{-2}(\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}})\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\|\Sigma^{-2}\|\|(\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\|\Sigma^{-2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{n^{3/2}\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}.

(F.14)

which satisfies the same bound as
\alpha_{1}
. Here we have noted that
\|\mathbf{W}_{*}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\|\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{K^{2}}
by (
F.12
) and the second conclusion of
Theorem

A.2
.

•

The term
\alpha_{3}
: By (
F.2
) and (
F.11
), with high probability we have that

\displaystyle\|\alpha_{3}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle=\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}(\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2})\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}\|(\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\sqrt{L\log(n)}\|(\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}\sqrt{L\log(n)}}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\mathcal{B}.

(F.15)

•

The term
\alpha_{4}
: We note that

\displaystyle\|\alpha_{4}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle=\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}})\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\|_{2,\infty}\|\sin\Theta(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim Kn\sqrt{L\log(n)}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\|\sin\Theta(\mathbf{\widehat{U}},\mathbf{U})\|\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim Kn\sqrt{L\log(n)}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\mathcal{A}\frac{K}{nL\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\asymp\mathcal{A}\frac{K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

(F.16)

Combining (
F.13
), (
F.14
), (
F.15
), and (
F.16
), we have that with probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
,

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{E})^{\top}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-2}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\underbrace{\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{n^{3/2}\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}}_{\beta_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\frac{K^{2}\sqrt{L\log(n)}}{\sqrt{n}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\mathcal{B}}_{\beta_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\frac{K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}\mathcal{A}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}}_{\beta_{3}}.

The proof is complete if we can argue that each of the three quantities
\beta_{i}
above are smaller than
c_{0}\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}}
for some sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
.

•

The quantity
\beta_{1}
:
First, note that
2
implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}{\log(n)K^{8}},

provided the constant
C
in the assumption is sufficiently large.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{n^{3/2}\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}\max_{l}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{n^{3/2}\sqrt{L}}c_{0}\frac{\max_{l}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}}{\log(n)K^{8}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\frac{n\max_{l}(\theta^{(l)}_{\max})^{2}}{n^{3/2}\sqrt{L}\sqrt{\log(n)}K^{5}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nL\log(n)}K^{5}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}}.

•

The quantity
\beta_{2}
:
By
2
,

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\frac{K^{2}\sqrt{L\log(n)}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathcal{B}\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{2}\bar{\lambda}}{\log(n)K^{8}}

\displaystyle\lesssim c_{0}\frac{\sqrt{Ln}\bar{\lambda}}{\sqrt{\log(n)}K^{6}}\bigg(\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\big(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}{nL^{3/2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{4}\log(n)\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{K^{3}\sqrt{\log(n)}\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}}+\frac{K^{2}\alpha_{\max}}{nL\bar{\lambda}^{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\asymp c_{0}\frac{(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2})^{1/2}}{K^{3}L\sqrt{n}\bar{\lambda}}+c_{0}\frac{\sqrt{\log(n)}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{K^{2}\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{\alpha_{\max}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}}{K^{3}\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}}+\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\sqrt{\log(n)}\sqrt{nL}\bar{\lambda}K^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\frac{1}{K^{3}L\sqrt{n}}+c_{0}\frac{1}{K^{2}\sqrt{nL}}+\frac{1}{K^{3}\sqrt{nL}}+\frac{1}{K^{4}\sqrt{nL}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}},

which follows from algebra similar to the proof of
Theorem

A.3
(e.g.,(
E.6
)) which shows that

\displaystyle\max\bigg\{\frac{1}{L\bar{\lambda}}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2},\frac{\sqrt{\log(n)}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}},\frac{\alpha_{\max}}{\bar{\lambda}}\bigg\}\lesssim 1.

•

The quantity
\beta_{3}
:
By (
E.9
), it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{K^{8}\log(n)}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\leq c_{0}\bar{\lambda}^{2},

and hence,

\displaystyle\beta_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{K^{2}\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\sqrt{L}\bar{\lambda}}\mathcal{A}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\|\mathrm{SNR}^{-1}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim c_{0}\frac{1}{K^{2}\sqrt{L}}\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\lesssim c_{0}\frac{1}{K^{3}\sqrt{L}},

which follows from the fact that
\mathcal{A}\lesssim\frac{1}{K}
as discussed previously. From our assumption that
L\geq n/K^{7}
, we see that
\frac{1}{K^{3}\sqrt{L}}\leq c_{0}\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}}
.

Therefore, combining these arguments and reassigning constants if necessary, the proof is complete.
∎

Appendix G
Proof of Minimax Lower Bound (Theorem
3.4
)

Proof.

Our proof mimics that of
Gao et al. 2018
;
Han et al. 2021
. For technical convenience we assume that
n
is divisible by
KL
and that the communities are equal-sized.

We now proceed in steps.

•

Step 1: Reduction to fundamental testing problem.
First, let
z\in[K]^{n}
be such that

\displaystyle c\frac{n}{K}=n_{1}=n_{2}\leq n_{3}\leq\cdots\leq n_{K},

with
n_{k}=|\mathcal{C}_{k}|
. In addition, let
\mathbf{B}^{(l)}
be the matrix
\mathbf{I}+\frac{\lambda_{\min}-1}{K}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
, which ensures that
\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}=\lambda_{\min}
. We claim that there exists
\theta^{(l)}
such that
\frac{\|\theta^{(l)}_{\mathcal{C}_{k}}\|^{2}}{K}
is the same for all
l\in[L]
and
k\in[K]
. In this manner we have that
\mathbf{P}^{(1)},\cdots\mathbf{P}^{(L)}\in\mathcal{P}(\lambda_{\min},K,n,\theta,L)
. We will verify the existence of
\theta^{(l)}
at the end of the proof.

For each
a\in[K]
, let
T_{a}
be a subset of
\mathcal{C}_{a}
with cardinality
|T_{a}|=\lceil n_{a}-\frac{cn}{4K^{2}}\rceil
. Let
T=\cup_{a\in[K]}T_{a}
and define

\mathcal{Z}_{T}:=\{z^{\prime}:c\frac{n}{K}\leq|\{j\in[n]:z_{j}^{\prime}=a\}|\leq C\frac{n}{K}\text{ for all }a\in[K],z_{j}^{\prime}=z_{j}\text{ for all }j\in T\}.

If
\tilde{z}\neq z
and
\tilde{z}\in\mathcal{Z}_{T}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\{\tilde{z}_{j}\neq z_{j}\}\leq\frac{1}{n}|T^{c}|\leq\frac{K}{n}\frac{cn}{4K^{2}}=\frac{c}{4K}.

Similarly, if
\pi
is any non-identity permutation on
[K]
it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\{\pi(\tilde{z}_{j})\neq z_{j}\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\min_{a}|T_{a}|\geq\frac{1}{n}\bigg(\frac{cn}{K}-\frac{cn}{4K^{2}}\bigg)\geq\frac{3c}{4K}.

Therefore for any
\tilde{z}\in\mathcal{Z}_{T}
, it holds that the identity permutation is the optimal permutation.

Therefore, following
Gao et al. 2018
, Theorem 2, it holds that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{z}}\sup_{z}\mathbb{E}\ell(\widehat{z},z)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c}{6K|T^{c}|}\sum_{j\in T^{c}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2K^{2}}\inf_{\widehat{z}_{j}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}(\widehat{z}_{j}=1)+\mathbb{P}_{H_{1}}(\widehat{z}_{j}=0)\big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\geq C^{\prime}\frac{1}{K^{3}|T^{c}|}\sum_{j\in T^{c}}\bigg[\inf_{\widehat{z}_{j}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}(\widehat{z}_{j}=1)+\mathbb{P}_{H_{1}}(\widehat{z}_{j}=0)\big)\bigg]

where
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
are the distributions given
z_{j}
is in community 1 or 2 respectively.

•

Step 2: Lower bounding the node-wise Type I and Type II error
. We next note that the quantity
\inf_{\widehat{z}_{j}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}(\widehat{z}_{j}=1)+\mathbb{P}_{H_{1}}(\widehat{z}_{j}=0)\big)
is the sum of the type I and type II error for the simple-simple hypothesis test. By standard testing results, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\widehat{z}(i)=2|z(i)=1\big)+\mathbb{P}\big[\widehat{z}(i)=1|z(i)=2\big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}d_{KL}(P_{0}||P_{1})},

where
d_{KL}
is the K-L divergence between
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
. We claim that with our particular choice of
\theta^{(l)}
we can demonstrate that

\displaystyle d_{KL}(P_{0}||P_{1})

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\lambda_{\min}^{2}\theta_{\max}^{2}\frac{n}{L}<1.

If this is the case, by combining our arguments and noting that the errors are all the same across all nodes, we complete the proof. Therefore, it remains to verify the existence of
\theta^{(l)}
satisfying the requisite inequalities.

•

Details of the construction.
We now explain the construction of
\theta^{(l)}
, which we will assign differently according to each node and network. Recall that we assume that each community is of exact size
\frac{n}{K}
. Within each community, divide the nodes into subsets of equal size
\frac{n}{KL}
, so that each community
k
is partitioned into
L
different subsets
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{k},\cdots\mathcal{S}^{k}_{L}
. We will assign each degree correction within each subset to be either
\theta_{\max}
or
\theta_{\min}=\frac{\theta_{\max}}{L}
depending on which network it belongs to such that the degree correction parameter for the nodes in
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{k}
are equal to
\theta_{\max}
within network
l
and otherwise are equal to
\theta_{\min}
.
In this manner each vertex has
\theta_{\max}
as its degree correction parameter exactly once across all the networks, and
\theta_{\min}=\frac{\theta_{\max}}{L}
as its degree correction parameter
L-1
times. Note that

\displaystyle\|\theta\|_{1}

\displaystyle=K\bigg(\frac{n}{KL}\theta_{\max}+\frac{n(L-1)}{K}\theta_{\min}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{L}\theta_{\max}+\frac{n(L-1)}{L}\frac{\theta_{\max}}{L}

\displaystyle=\theta_{\max}\frac{n}{L}\bigg(2-\frac{1}{L}\bigg);

\displaystyle\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=K\bigg(\frac{n}{KL}\theta_{\max}^{2}+\frac{n(L-1)}{KL^{2}}\theta_{\min}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=K\bigg(\frac{n}{KL}\theta_{\max}^{2}+\frac{n(L-1)}{KL^{3}}\theta_{\max}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\theta_{\max}^{2}\frac{n}{L}\bigg(1+\frac{L-1}{L^{2}}\bigg).

Consequently, our assumption implies that

\displaystyle c_{1}\leq\frac{\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}}{\|\theta\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta_{\max}^{2}\frac{n}{L}}{\theta_{\max}^{4}\frac{n^{2}}{L^{2}}\lambda_{\min}^{2}}\frac{2-\frac{1}{L}}{(1+\frac{L-1}{L^{2}})^{2}}=\frac{1}{\theta_{\max}^{2}\frac{n}{L}\lambda_{\min}^{2}}\big(2+o(1)\big).

This implies that

\displaystyle\theta_{\max}^{2}\frac{n}{L}\lambda_{\min}^{2}\leq\frac{2+o(1)}{c_{1}}.

As long as
c_{1}\geq 10
, we have that
\theta_{\max}^{2}\frac{n}{L}\lambda_{\min}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}
. Next, the KL-divergence between two Bernoulli distributions with parameters
a
and
b
is governed by

\displaystyle d_{KL}(a,b)

\displaystyle=a\log(a/b)+(1-a)\log\frac{1-a}{1-b}\leq\frac{(a-b)^{2}}{b(1-b)}.

By the product property of independent tests, we have that as long as
\lambda_{\min}\leq\frac{1}{2}
,

\displaystyle d_{KL}(P_{0}||P_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{1}^{(l)}-\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{1}^{(l)}(1-\lambda_{\min}))^{2}}{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{1}^{(l)}(1-\lambda_{\min})(1-\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{1}^{(l)}(1-\lambda_{\min}))}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{1}^{(l)})^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{2}}{\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{1}^{(l)}(1-\lambda_{\min})(1-\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{1}^{(l)}(1-\lambda_{\min}))}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_{i}^{(l)}\theta_{1}^{(l)}\frac{(\lambda_{\min})^{2}}{1-\lambda_{\min}}

\displaystyle\leq 4\|\theta\|_{1}\lambda_{\min}^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\theta_{1}^{(l)}

\displaystyle\leq 4\|\theta\|_{1}\lambda_{\min}^{2}\bigg(\theta_{\max}+(L-1)\theta_{\min}\bigg)

\displaystyle=4\theta_{\max}^{2}\frac{n}{L}\big(2-\frac{1}{L}\big)\lambda_{\min}^{2}\bigg(1+\frac{(L-1)}{L}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 16\theta_{\max}^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{2}\frac{n}{L}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}.

This verifies our main condition.

We also need to check that our condition on the sparsity holds. We have that

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\log(n+L)}}{\theta_{\max}^{2}\frac{n}{L}\lambda_{\min}^{2}}\ll\sqrt{L}\iff\theta_{\max}\lambda_{\min}\gg\frac{L^{1/4}}{\sqrt{n}}\log^{1/4}(n+L).

Therefore, since
\lambda_{\min}\asymp 1
we may take
\theta_{\max}\asymp\frac{L^{1/4+\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{n}}\log^{1/4}(n+L)
.

This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix H
Further simulations and theory for single network spherical clustering

In the main paper, we have compared our results to the best-known expected misclustering error for spectral clustering without refinement for degree-corrected stochastic blockmodels; i.e., the result in
Jin et al. 2022b
.
However, DC-MASE uses the spherical normalization, and the result in
Jin et al. 2022b
uses the SCORE normalization. While
Jin et al. 2022b
demonstrate that the SCORE procedure exhibits an exponential misclustering rate, to the best of our knowledge there is no similarly strong error rate for vanilla spectral clustering with the spherical normalization, though there are polynomial upper bounds
(
Lei and Rinaldo 2015
;
Qin and Rohe 2013
)
, as well as some perfect clustering results
(
Lyzinski et al. 2014
;
Su et al. 2020
)
.
Conveniently, as a byproduct of our analysis we characterize the rows of
\mathbf{\widehat{Y}}^{(l)}
,and we are able to apply the same proof strategy for
Theorem

3.1
to analyze the result of running
K
-means on these rows.
The following theorem demonstrates an exponential error rate for single network clustering. For simplicity, we suppress the dependence of the parameters on the index
l
.

Theorem H.1

(Single Network Misclustering Rate: Spherical Normalization)
.

Assume that
1
and
2
hold (with
\bar{\lambda}=\lambda_{\min}
).
Then the output of
(1+\varepsilon)

K
-means on the rows of
\mathbf{\widehat{Y}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\ell(\widehat{z},z)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2K}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\bigg(-c\theta_{i}\min\bigg\{\frac{\|\theta\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{2}}{K^{3}\|\theta\|_{3}^{3}},\frac{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}}{K^{3/2}\theta_{\max}}\bigg\}\bigg)+O(n^{-10}).

This rate exactly matches the rate obtained in
Jin et al. 2022b
, but the assumptions are somewhat different, which we now describe. First, the signal-strength assumption in
Jin et al. 2022b
requires that

\displaystyle\frac{K^{8}\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}{\|\theta\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{2}}\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)^{2}\lesssim 1.

In contrast, we require that

\displaystyle\frac{K^{8}\theta_{\max}\|\theta\|_{1}\log(n)}{\|\theta\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{3}}\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

which is weaker whenever
\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}}\lesssim\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg).

This regime corresponds to high degree heterogeneity relative to the community separation. For example, if the network is sparse (e.g.
\|\theta\|\asymp\sqrt{\log(n)}
), then it must be that
\lambda_{\min}\asymp 1
(or else the assumption fails).

One possible reason that the spherical normalization requires weaker conditions on the degree heterogeneity is that the spherical normalization is more “robust” to severe degree heterogeneity as it uses all the eigenvectors simultaneously to normalize, whereas the SCORE-based approach only uses a single eigenvector. In essence, the standard deviation of the leading eigenvector exhibits additional dependence on
\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}
, but no dependence on
\lambda_{\min}
, whereas the standard deviation of the spherical normalization does not depend as strongly on
\theta_{\max}/\theta_{\min}
, but has additional dependence on
\lambda_{\min}
.

Furthermore,
Jin et al. 2022b
impose an additional (perhaps artificial) assumption on the leading eigenvalue and eigenvector of the matrix
K\|\theta\|^{-2}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{\Theta}^{2}\mathbf{Z})^{1/2}\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{\Theta}^{2}\mathbf{Z})^{1/2}
; namely that the leading eigenvalue is well separated from the remaining eigenvalues and the leading eigenvector has entries of similar order. From a technical perspective, such a condition is required so that the SCORE procedure (which uses the entries of the leading eigenvector of the adjacency matrix for its normalization) does not “explode.” However, such a condition may be unintuitive. Consider, for example, the case that all
\theta_{i}
’s are equal and that
\mathbf{B}
is of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&.5&0\\
.5&1&b\\
0&b&1\end{pmatrix}.

Assuming that all the communities are of equal size, the leading eigenvector is of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=\big(\frac{1}{2b},\frac{\sqrt{1+4b^{2}}}{2b},1\big)^{\top}

with corresponding eigenvalue
\frac{1}{2}\big(\sqrt{4b^{2}+1}+2\big)
. As
b\to 0
, the assumption in
Jin et al. 2022b
is violated as the leading two entries of
\mathbf{v}
diverge. While such a setting is perhaps slightly contrived (as the third community is significantly easier to separate), the assumption imposed on the leading eigenvector in
Jin et al. 2022b
fails to accommodate this scenario. In contrast, the spherical normalization is able to handle such scenarios. In essence, the reason for this difference is that the SCORE normalization requires estimating the leading eigenvector with high fidelity, which depends on the gap between the leading eigenvalue and the bottom
K-1
eigenvalues. While the Perron-Frobenius Theorem shows that there is necessarily
some
separation, the additional assumption imposed by
Jin et al. 2022b
ensures that that this separation is sufficiently strong. The spherical normalization does not require such a separation as it uses all eigenvectors simultaneously.

In summary, we see that the assumptions imposed by the spherical normalization require a) slightly weaker assumptions on the degree heterogeneity, b) slightly stronger assumptions on the smallest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}
, and c) no additional assumptions ensuring that the leading eigenvector is well-separated.

H.1
Simulations for single network clustering

In this section, we study the role of spherical normalization versus other normalization procedures in the DCSBM via simulated data.

Given an adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}
, write its eigendecomposition as

\mathbf{A}=\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}+\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{\perp}\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}_{\perp}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{\perp},

where
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}\in\real^{K\times K}
is a diagonal matrix containing the
K
leading eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}
(in magnitude) and
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\in\real^{n\times K}
is a matrix containing the corresponding
K
leading eigenvectors. The methods we consider in the simulations are (1)
spherical
spectral clustering using the scaled matrix of eigenvectors
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}|\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}|^{1/2}
, (2)
spherical unscaled
spectral clustering, where we consider the matrix of eigenvectors
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
, (3) the
SCORE
normalization as proposed by
Jin 2015
, (4) the SCORE+ method of
Jin et al. 2022b
, and (5) the
unthresholded SCORE
method of
Jin 2015
. We note that
Jin 2015
proposes a thresholding approach to remove the low-degree vertices, and provides a theoretical analysis of this step. In principle, implementing a thresholding approach like this can potentially improve methods that use the spherical normalization as well, but we decided to include the un-thresholded SCORE in the simulations to observe the effect of the embedding methodology directly without further removal of low-degree nodes. All variations of the SCORE methods are computed using the
ScorePlus
R package
(
Jin et al. 2022a
)
.

All networks in the simulations have
n=300
vertices and
K=3
communities. Unless explicitly indicated, we consider the following parameters:

\mathbf{B}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\gamma&\gamma\\
\gamma&1&\gamma\\
\gamma&\gamma&1\end{array}\right),\quad\quad\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{1}_{n/K}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{K},\quad\quad\quad\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\text{Uniform}(0.1,1),

\mathbf{\Theta}=\text{diag}(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}).

The entries of the symmetric adjacency matrix are sampled independently with probabilities given by
\mathbf{P}=\alpha\mathbf{\Theta}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{\Theta}
, where
\alpha
is a constant adjusted to make the average expected degree of
\mathbf{P}
equal to 15.
The particular simulation scenarios considered are as follows:

•

Between-community connectivity
: the off-diagonal values of
\mathbf{B}
are varied from
0
to
0.7
.

•

Community imbalance:
we change the value of
\mathbf{B}_{11}
to increase the connectivity of the first community.

•

Community sizes:
vertex memberships are assigned independently at random with probabilities
(1/3+\epsilon,1/3-\frac{\epsilon}{3},1/3-\frac{2\epsilon}{3})
, with
\epsilon\in[

•

Degree distribution power
: the degree-correction parameters are simulated as
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}[U(0.1,1)]^{p}
, i.e., uniformly distributed random variables raised to the power of
p\geq 0
.

The simulation results are shown in Figure
H.1
. The results confirm the theoretical analysis, showing that the spherical normalization is more robust to degree heterogeneity than the SCORE normalization, as demonstrated in the performance with respect to changes in the degree distribution power, but this later one has better performance in terms of community magnitudes and community sizes.

Figure H.1
:
Adjusted Rand index (ARI) of different normalization strategies for single network spectral clustering. Values close to one indicate agreement with the true communities

H.2
Scaled vs. unscaled spherical spectral clustering

This paper mainly considers scaled eigenvectors for spectral clustering in single and multilayer networks. Namely, step 1(a) of Algorithm
1
uses the matrix of scaled eigenvectors given by
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{(l)}=\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{(l)}|\widehat{\Lambda}^{(l)}|^{1/2}
.
Theorem

3.1
provides an upper bound for the misclustering error rate of a version of single-layer spherical spectral clustering using this scaled matrix
before normalizing its rows and clustering via
K
-means.
Alternatively, one might consider the unscaled eigenvector matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{(l)}
followed by row-normalization and clustering
(
Lei and Rinaldo 2015
;
Qin and Rohe 2013
)
. While both approaches can estimate the clusters consistently, we argue that the scaled matrix can alleviate the effect of different community sizes in the performance of spectral clustering. This property can be explained by the interpretation of the embeddings as latent positions of a generalized random dot product graph, which are invariant to community sizes.

To explain the intuition behind the use of scaled eigenvectors, let
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{\Theta}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{\Theta}
with
\mathbf{Z}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times K}
be the probability matrix of a DCSBM with
K
communities, and connectivity matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\real^{K\times K}
with
\mathbf{B}_{kk}=1,k\in[K]
. It can be checked that the matrix of
K
leading eigenvectors of
\mathbf{P}
, denoted by
\mathbf{U}\in\real^{n\times K}
, has the form

\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{\Theta}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{T}^{-1/2}\mathbf{Q}

for some orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{Q}\in\real^{K\times K}
and with
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{\Theta}^{2}\mathbf{Z}
a
K\times K
diagonal matrix. In this case, for a given row of
\mathbf{U}
, say
i\in[n]
, if
\mathbf{Z}_{ik}=1
, then
\|\mathbf{U}_{i,\cdot}\|=\theta_{i}\mathbf{T}^{-1/2}_{kk}=\theta_{i}/\|\mathbf{\Theta}\mathbf{Z}e_{k}\|
, where
e_{k}\in\real^{K}
is the standard unit vector. Thus, the norms of the rows of
\mathbf{U}
depend on the community size,
n_{k}
, as well as the magnitude of the degree correction parameters for vertices in the community. On the other hand, the
i
-th row of
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{U}|\mathbf{\Lambda}|^{1/2}
has norm given by
\|\mathbf{X}_{i,\cdot}\|=\theta_{i}
, which can be verified by observing that
\mathbf{X}_{i,\cdot}\mathbf{X}_{i,\cdot}^{\top}=\mathbf{P}_{ii}=\theta_{i}^{2}
. Thus, we argue that the row-normalization in spherical spectral clustering is more prone to affect the clustering error in the unscaled eigenvector case when the communities have different sizes, as the variance of this normalization has a different order.

Figure
H.2
shows an illustration of the effect of different community sizes in the embeddings obtained by the normalized rows of
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
(left panel)
and
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}
(right panel), demonstrating that the variance of the point clouds of vectors corresponding to the larger communities can be much larger than the
ones in smaller communities, which might yield poor clustering performance. More specifically, a single graph is generated from the DCSBM model with
n=800
,
K=4
and connectivity matrix
\mathbf{B}=(1-\gamma)\mathbf{I}_{K}+\gamma\mathbf{1}_{K}\mathbf{1}_{K}^{\top}
, for
\gamma=0.4
. The degree correction parameters are generated at random as
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}U(0.8,0.1)
and the community memberships are assigned at random with probabilities
\left(\frac{5}{11},\frac{5}{11},\frac{1}{22},\frac{1}{22}\right)
. In the left panel (row-normalized eigenvectors of
\mathbf{A}
), the spread of the larger clusters (corresponding to communities 1 and 2) dominates, which results in these communities being partitioned into half by the
K
-means algorithm. The right panel (row-normalized scaled eigenvectors of
\mathbf{A}
) shows that scaling alleviates the effect of different community sizes, as all point clouds show a similar spread. In this particular simulation, the misclustering error in the unscaled eigenvectors is 0.205, whereas the scaled eigenvectors recover the communities perfectly. We repeated this experiment for different values of
\gamma
in
[0.1,0.8]
and averaged the results of 10 simulations (see Table
H.1
, observing a superior performance in clustering using the scaled eigenvectors for a wide range of values of
\gamma
.

Figure H.2
:

\left(\frac{5}{11},\frac{5}{11},\frac{1}{22},\frac{1}{22}\right)
.

H.3
Proof of Theorem
H.1

Proof of
Theorem

H.1
.

The proof of this result is similar to the proof of the main result. First we demonstrate the initial error implies that each community contains at least
\frac{3}{4}

of its true members, whereupon we study the empirical centroids and show that they are closer to their true cluster centroid than they are to each other. Finally, instead of applying
Theorem

A.3
to obtain the exponential error rate we apply
Theorem

A.1
. As this result only involves a single network, we suppress the dependency on
l
for ease of notation.

Step 1: Initial Hamming Error

Observe that
\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{M_{Y}},
where it straightforward to check that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}\leq\|\big(\mathbf{M_{Y}}\big)_{r\cdot}-\big(\mathbf{M_{Y}}\big)_{s\cdot}\|\leq 2.

The upper bound is immediate; as for the lower bound, we may apply the same argument as in the proof of
Lemma

A.2
. Let the matrix
\mathbf{\widehat{X}}:=\mathbf{\widehat{Z}}\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{\mathbf{Y}}
, where
\mathbf{\widehat{Z}}
and
\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{\mathbf{Y}}
are the outputs of
(1+\varepsilon)

K
-means on the rows of
\mathbf{\widehat{Y}}
, and let
S_{r}:=\{i\in\mathcal{C}(r):\|\mathbf{W}_{*}\mathbf{\widehat{X}}_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{Y}_{i\cdot}\|\geq\delta_{r}/2\}
, where
\delta_{r}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}
. By Lemma 5.3 of
Lei and Rinaldo 2015
and a similar argument as in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{z}(i)\neq\mathcal{P}(z(i))\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\varepsilon}}{\lambda_{\min}}\|\mathbf{\widehat{Y}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{Y}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\varepsilon}n}{\lambda_{\min}}\|\mathbf{\widehat{Y}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{Y}\|_{2,\infty}^{2}.

By
Corollary

A.1
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-15})
it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\widehat{Y}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{Y}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}}{\theta_{\min}}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{K\sqrt{\log(n)}}{\|\theta\|\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{K^{2}\theta_{\max}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|_{1}}{\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}\|\theta^{(l)}\|^{4}}\bigg(\log(n)\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}^{(l)}}+\bigg(\frac{\theta_{\max}^{(l)}}{\theta_{\min}^{(l)}}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{K^{5/2}\log(n)}{(\lambda_{\min}^{(l)})^{1/2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{8\sqrt{C_{\varepsilon}K}}\lambda_{\min},

where
\beta\in(0,1]

is such that
n_{\min}\geq\beta n_{\max}
, and where the final bound holds under the conditions of
Theorem

H.1
.
Let this event be denoted
\mathcal{E}
. By squaring the above bound we arrive at

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{z}(i)\neq\mathcal{P}(z(i))\}

\displaystyle\leq n\frac{\beta^{2}}{64K}\lambda_{\min}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{64}\lambda_{\min}n_{\min}.

Therefore, each cluster is associated to a true cluster, denoted as
\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)
, where
|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|\geq(1-\beta\lambda_{\min}/64)n_{\min}
and
|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)\setminus\mathcal{C}(r)|\leq\frac{\beta\lambda_{\min}}{64}n_{\min}
. Note that since
\beta\in(0,1)
and
\lambda_{\min}\in(0,1)
, then
\beta\lambda_{\min}/64<1

this is a well-defined fraction.

Step 2: Properties of Empirical Centroids

Recall that we denote
(\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{\mathbf{Y}})_{r\cdot}
and
(\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{Y}})_{r\cdot}
as the cluster centroids for
\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)
and
\mathcal{C}(r)
respectively.
Then by a similar argument as in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{W}_{*}(\mathbf{\widehat{M}}_{\mathbf{Y}})_{r\cdot}-(\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{Y}})_{r\cdot}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|^{1/2}}\|\mathbf{\widehat{Y}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{Y}\|_{F}+2\frac{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)\setminus\mathcal{C}(r)|}{|\mathcal{\widehat{C}}(r)|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\min}(1-\beta\lambda_{\min}/64)}}\sqrt{n}\|\mathbf{\widehat{Y}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top}-\mathbf{Y}\|_{2,\infty}+2\frac{\beta n_{\min}\lambda_{\min}}{64(1-\beta\lambda_{\min}/64)n_{\min}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\min}\beta(1-\beta\lambda_{\min}/64)}}\sqrt{n}\frac{\beta}{8\sqrt{C_{\varepsilon}K}}\lambda_{\min}+\frac{\lambda_{\min}}{32(1-\beta\lambda_{\min}/64)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\min}\beta(1-\beta\lambda_{\min}/64)}}\sqrt{\frac{Kn_{\min}}{\beta}}\frac{\beta}{8\sqrt{C_{\varepsilon}K}}\lambda_{\min}+\frac{\lambda_{\min}}{32(1-\beta\lambda_{\min}/64)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}},

since
\lambda_{\min}\in(0,1)
by assumption. The above bound holds on the event
\mathcal{E}
.

Step 3: Applying The Asymptotic Expansion

Arguing similarly as in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\ell(\widehat{z},z)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{Z}_{i\cdot}\neq\mathbf{\widehat{Z}}_{i\cdot},\mathcal{E}\big)+O(n^{-15}).

On the event
\mathcal{E}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Stage\ I}}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}},

and hence by repeating the arguments in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Z}_{i\cdot}\neq\mathbf{\widehat{Z}}_{i\cdot},\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\|(\mathbf{\widehat{Y}}\mathbf{W}_{*}^{\top})_{i\cdot}-\mathbf{Y}_{i\cdot}\|\geq\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}},\mathcal{E}\big)

\displaystyle\leq K\max_{k}\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\big|e_{i}^{\top}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E})\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}e_{k}\big|\geq\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}/K}\bigg\}.

Here
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E})
is the linear term from
Theorem

A.1
with
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{P}
. We now apply Bernstein’s inequality. The variance
v
is upper bounded by

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j}\theta_{i}\theta_{j}\|e_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{U}^{(l)}\|^{2}\||\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\|^{2}\|\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j}\theta_{i}\theta_{j}\frac{\theta_{j}^{2}K}{\|\theta\|^{2}}\frac{K}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}}\frac{1}{\theta_{i}^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K^{2}\|\theta\|_{3}^{3}}{\|\theta\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}\theta_{i}}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\max_{j}\|e_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{U}^{(l)}\|\||\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\|\|\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{X}_{i\cdot})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\theta_{j}\frac{K}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}}\frac{1}{\theta_{i}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K\theta_{\max}}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}\theta_{i}}.

By Bernstein’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\big|e_{i}^{\top}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{E})\mathbf{U}^{(l)}|\Lambda^{(l)}|^{-1/2}\mathbf{I}_{p,q}e_{k}\big|\geq\frac{1}{8}\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}/K}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\bigg(-\frac{\frac{1}{128}\frac{\lambda_{\min}}{K}}{C\frac{K^{2}\|\theta\|_{3}^{3}}{\|\theta\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}\theta_{i}}+\frac{\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}}{24\sqrt{K}}C\frac{K\theta_{\max}}{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}\theta_{i}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\bigg(-c\theta_{i}\min\bigg\{\frac{\|\theta\|^{4}\lambda_{\min}^{2}}{K^{3}\|\theta\|_{3}^{3}},\frac{\|\theta\|^{2}\lambda_{\min}}{K^{3/2}\theta_{\max}}\bigg\}\bigg).

Assembling everything together completes the proof.
∎

Appendix I
Additional simulation experiments for multilayer networks

We evaluate the performance of different multilayer community detection methods in terms of the sparsity of the networks. We use the same simulation settings described in
Section

4
, but here we fix the number of layers as
L=20
and
n=150
, and we change the value of the edge density, which is controlled by
\alpha^{(l)}
. In particular, we change the value of this parameter in order to obtain a specific expected edge density, defined as
\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i,j}\mathbf{P}^{(l)}
. This value of the edge density is changed in the range
[2/150,24/150]
.

The results of this simulation are shown in Figure
I.1
. For a given edge density (x-axis), a point representing the average misclustering error of 100 simulation results using a given method is plotted. The results show that DC-MASE is always able to estimate the communities correctly if the networks are sufficiently dense. For the scenarios considered and the range of edge density values, there is no other method that is able to always perform perfect clustering. The only other method that always improves its performance with more density is graph-tool. This might be expected from the fact that this method uses the correct likelihood for the model, but DC-MASE substantially outperforms the method in the last column. Besides, DC-MASE is computationally more scalable than graph-tool.

Figure I.1
:
Misclustering error rate of different community detection methods as a function of the edge density of the networks. The number of layers is fixed as
L=20
, and the simulation scenarios contemplate different types of heterogeneity in the parameters, as described in Section
I
.

Appendix J
Additional data results

J.1
Out-of-sample performance and robustness to choice of
K

We compared the performance of DC-MASE with the other spectral clustering algorithms considered in
Section

4
. In the absence of ground truth communities, we measure the performance in terms of out-of-sample mean squared error (MSE) for a given graph
l
and some number of communities
K
, defined as

\text{MSE}(K,l)=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\|\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\widehat{\mathbf{P}}^{(l)}_{\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(-l,K)}}\|_{F}^{2}.

Here,
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(-l,K)}
indicates the estimated community memberships obtained from a particular method fitted on the set of graphs indexed by
[L]\setminus\{l\}
with
K
communities. Given
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
, the value of the expected adjacency matrix is estimated as
\widehat{\mathbf{P}}^{(l)}_{\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}}=\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}_{\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}}{\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}}\widehat{\mathbf{B}}^{(l)}_{\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}}{\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}_{\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}}
, where
\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{(l)}_{\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}^{(l)}_{\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}}
are the plug-in estimates defined via Eq. (
5.1
) using the communities defined by
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
. As the expected value of the average MSE is minimized by the expected adjacency matrices calculated with the correct communities, small values of this quantity are a proxy for the quality of the community estimates.

After calculating the MSE for all the graphs in the data and for different values of
K
, we performed a paired comparison via the MSE difference between the results for a given method and DC-MASE for each value of
K
and
l
. Figure
J.1
shows boxplots of these differences across all values of
l\in[L]
and as a function of the number of communities. Notably, the MSE differences are positive for almost all graphs in the data and all values of
K
, indicating that the communities obtained by DC-MASE generally have smaller generalization error than the ones obtained by the other spectral methods considered.

Figure J.1
:
Paired out-of-sample mean squared error (MSE) difference for the Frobenius error of the estimated expected adjacency matrices obtained by each method and DC-MASE. Positive values indicate that the MSE of the respective method is larger than the MSE of DC-MASE.

J.2
On the common community membership assumption

To validate a multilayer DCSBM with common community memberships across time in the airport network data, we compare the community memberships in each month. For this goal, we fit community memberships for each layer (month) independently using our method on a single network (assuming
K=4
, as estimated before). We then compare the community memberships recovered by each layer with the overall community memberships using our joint spectral clustering algorithm with all the layers together. Figure
J.2
shows the percentage of nodes with different memberships on each clustering result (monthly vs overall) for each month. As can be noticed, most of the months before the start of the pandemic (month 50, corresponding to February 2020) were in close agreement with the communities recovered by the joint clustering. During this period, the percentage of difference usually ranges between 5% to 10%. However, when the pandemic started, this percentage rose up to 33%, and went down again by the end of the period of study. This suggests that a model with constant communities across the layers is at least reasonable before the pandemic. After the pandemic started, the number of flights generally decreased, making the networks sparser and, hence, increasing the uncertainty in the membership estimates. Nevertheless, Theorem
3.3
suggests that even if there are changes in the community memberships, our method is still able to recover a common clustering structure for the majority of the networks.

Figure J.2
:
Proportion of nodes with different community memberships on a model fit in a single month compared with the community memberships obtained by our algorithm for joint spectral clustering with all months. Overall, most months before February 2020 (red vertical line) show agreement in terms of community memberships.
